## INFRASTRUCTURE & PERFORMANCE

- 🟡 M — **Ratchet ESLint warnings to zero and enforce --max-warnings 0** — Follow-up to [[FIX-459]], which wired the ESLint presets across all packages + apps and promoted the tests.yml lint step to blocking by downgrading several high-volume rules from error to warn (so the gate could go green without ballooning that PR). The lint gate is now blocking but warnings are still allowed. Backlog at FIX-459 landing time: ~76 warnings — no-console x23, @typescript-eslint/no-unused-vars x21, @typescript-eslint/no-explicit-any x21, no-inner-declarations x5, react/no-unescaped-entities x2, @next/next/no-img-element x2, react/no-children-prop x1, react-hooks/exhaustive-deps x1. The ratchet: (1) drive the backlog to zero — remove/underscore unused vars, replace explicit any with real types, gate console behind a logger or allow-list, fix the genuine react/no-children-prop + exhaustive-deps sites; (2) re-promote the downgraded rules in packages/config/eslint/{base,next}.js from warn back to error as each category reaches zero; (3) add --max-warnings 0 to every package lint script (or 'next lint --max-warnings 0' for apps) so new warnings fail CI. Do it rule-by-rule to keep diffs reviewable. See packages/config/eslint/base.js + next.js for the current warn-level overrides flagged with FIX-459 comments. <!--id:FIX-460-->
- 🟡 S — **Wire egress to a kill switch — DEFERRED until an accurate billable-egress source exists** — Deferred by design (not a bug to fix now). [[FIX-602]] established that the dashboard egress figure is a raw-NIC UPPER BOUND written as source='estimated', not billable egress. Auto-tripping the connection_graph_live kill switch on that inflated proxy would false-fire every 10-min cron cycle — exactly the failure [[FIX-602]] removes. Supabase exposes no public Management API endpoint for billable egress (probed 2026-06-19; every egress endpoint 404s). When/if an accurate billable-egress source lands (undocumented endpoint appears, or a manual monthly figure is wired), add 'supabase.egress_bytes' to the connection_graph_live switch's metrics array (it already watches api_requests_7d + disk_used_bytes at auto_trip_threshold_pct 95) and flip the snapshot source back to 'api'. Until then this stays OPEN as a tracked dependency. <!--id:FIX-603-->
- 🟡 M — **Option 2 — splice live get_jurisdiction_activity over cached jurisdiction payload** — Deferred follow-up to [[FIX-663]]. The jurisdiction_page_cache materializes the FULL get_jurisdiction_page payload including the activity feed, so cached activity is up to ~1 day stale (option 1, agreed with Craig). If intra-day activity freshness becomes load-bearing, switch the wrapper to splice live get_jurisdiction_activity(p_id, 20) over the cached payload on a cache hit: read the cached jsonb, then jsonb_set the 'activity' key to a freshly-computed get_jurisdiction_activity result. Keeps the boundary/officials/institutions/spend sections served from cache (the expensive cold-IOWait parts FIX-663 fixed) while activity stays live. Only do this if a freshness complaint actually surfaces — the extra per-request activity query reintroduces some of the request-path cost FIX-663 removed. <!--id:FIX-670-->
- 🟢 S — **Moderation-harness pollution check doesn't watch auth.users/public.users** — The SF-P3 moderation harness's zero-pollution self-check (POLLUTION_TABLES in packages/data/src/pipelines/moderation-harness/index.ts) counts only 8 public tables before/after each run and does NOT watch auth.users or public.users. Its fixtures create ephemeral auth.users + public.users rows inside a rolled-back txn; if a rollback ever fails to take, leaked rows go undetected while the run still reports 'zero pollution'. That is exactly how ~30 bare-id auth.users rows leaked and poisoned admin.listUsers ([[FIX-660]]). FIX-660 hardened the three inserts (createUser / createAgedUser / createSyntheticUser) to be GoTrue-complete so a leak is now harmless, but leak DETECTION is still missing. Fix: add auth.users + public.users (schema-qualified counts) to the harness pollution watch so a future rollback failure surfaces loudly. Low priority — the harmless-leak hardening already shipped. <!--id:FIX-825-->
- 🟢 M — **Per-official chord RPCs ~7s cold for top-funded officials — optional per-official rollup** — [[FIX-780]] audit finding (docs/audits/2026-07-16-fix780-internal-agg-rpc-audit.md). The per-official chord RPCs (chord_industry_flows_for_official, chord_top_pacs_for_official, chord_donor_brackets_for_official) live-aggregate financial_relationships joined to financial_entities + entity_tags scoped to one official. Bounded per-official and served by the covering index financial_relationships_donor_rollup_idx, so warm cost is <4ms — BUT measured 6.85s COLD on prod for the single #1 most-funded official (2,831 donation rows; I/O-bound, read=10562 heap/index pages on the cache-starved Small), brushing the 8s service_role ceiling. Behind 24h withPublicCdnCache with 0 real calls in the 14-day window, so low priority. If it ever needs materializing: the industry-flows mode could reuse official_sector_dollars_mv ([[FIX-506]]) but that MV INNER JOINs entity_tags and so excludes the untagged bucket the RPC emits via LEFT JOIN — a semantics change, not a drop-in; top-pacs and donor-brackets have no existing MV at their grain and 0 traffic does not justify new per-official rollups. Cross-ref [[FIX-506]] [[FIX-775]] [[FIX-780]]. <!--id:FIX-839-->
- 🟢 M — **Cross-source FE dedup: multi-binding org clusters (edge-confirmed merge)** — Follow-up from FIX-544 (2026-07-16). FIX-544's org-only merge used an exactly-one-binding gate: it folds a same-name (canonical_name+entity_type) cluster only when a SINGLE source-bound row is the unambiguous survivor. Multi-binding clusters — a non-committee org whose cluster has >=2 source-bound rows (e.g. AMERICAN CHEMISTRY COUNCIL 'other' and CLUB FOR GROWTH 'other', each carrying two littlesis ids) — were deliberately excluded and remain as same-name duplicates on prod. To merge them safely, design a shared-edge / shared-identifier confirmation (overlap in external_relationships/financial_relationships neighbors, or a matching normalized website/EIN/CIK) to pick the true survivor rather than merging on name alone. Reuse the FIX-544 merge routine (data:merge-fe-collisions) FK surface + verification once a survivor is chosen. See docs/audits/2026-07-16-fix544-cross-source-collision-investigation.md. Cross-ref [[FIX-544]], [[FIX-271]], [[FIX-273]]. <!--id:FIX-840-->
- 🟠 M — **No trustworthy per-cycle 'last full FEC pass' watermark — attribution audits cannot ask 'is this row still endorsed by the current binding?'** — Blocking generalisation of [[FIX-954]], and referenced from its header (`packages/data/src/scripts/remediate-bound-cross-person.ts`). **The question every FEC attribution audit actually wants to ask is 'was this row written by the most recent full pass for its cycle?' — a row the current binding no longer produces is by definition mis-bound.** That question is not answerable today, and three separate attempts to derive an answer were measured and rejected on the local clone 2026-08-01: (1) **`max(updated_at)` per cycle** — marks **1,622,392 of 1,734,219 cycle-2024 rows stale**, because the recent runs were not full passes (the last full one was 2026-07-13 at 2,040,556 rows; 2026-07-19 wrote 251,902 and 2026-08-01 wrote 302,538). Useless as a boundary. (2) **`data_sync_log`** — has a `pipeline='fec_bulk'` row per run but **records no cycle scope at all** (`metadata` carries only `{peak_rss_mb}`), and the history is not clean: repeated `failed` rows through July and a row still stuck at `status='running'` from 2026-07-27 that no one reconciled. So 'last complete run covering cycle X' cannot be computed. (3) **Row timestamps as a proxy for pipeline writes** — invalid, because `financial_relationships` carries a `BEFORE UPDATE` trigger `financial_relationships_updated_at → set_updated_at()`, so ANY maintenance UPDATE bumps it. [[FIX-955]]'s repair moved 30,564 rows with `SET to_id = …` and stamped every one of them, which is indistinguishable afterwards from a pipeline write. **Consequence:** [[FIX-954]]'s absolute-staleness gate had to be re-expressed RELATIVE to the holder's own rows ('does this official have rows newer than its contaminated set?'). That is sound and it shipped, but it is strictly weaker — it needs the holder to still be actively written, so it cannot decide an official whose binding is legitimately dormant, and it cannot tell 'this cycle was never re-run' apart from 'this official stopped receiving'. It also means the rule silently degrades if a cycle goes unprocessed for a long stretch. **The fix is small and the hook already exists:** `pipeline_state.fec_bulk_run_state` ALREADY tracks per-cycle, per-stage progress (the pipeline logs `⟳ Pending fec_bulk_run_state: cycle=2026 … indiv-to-committee=0/514958 …` on startup). Extend it to record, per cycle and per stage, the timestamp of the last pass that ran to COMPLETION, and expose a helper (e.g. `fec_cycle_watermark(cycle int)`) the audits can join against. Then 'not endorsed by the current binding' is a direct comparison and [[FIX-954]] can drop its relative proxy. Two things to do alongside: reconcile the stuck `running` `data_sync_log` row and add a guard so an abandoned run cannot stay `running` indefinitely; and consider whether bulk maintenance scripts should suppress the `updated_at` trigger (or write a separate `last_pipeline_write_at`) so a row's provenance stays legible after a merge or repair. Cross-ref [[FIX-954]], [[FIX-955]], [[FIX-952]], [[FIX-930]], [[FIX-934]]. <!--id:FIX-957-->
- 🟠 M — **Seven pg_cron jobs blow the 6h statement_timeout; each blowout starves every other job that fires in its window (rule-taggers-weekly fails 4 of 5 runs)** — **Found while diagnosing [[FIX-968]]; the envelope data is filed at `docs/audits/2026-08-06-fix968-cron-window-envelopes.md`.** Measured across the full retained `cron.job_run_details` window (2026-06-29 → 08-06, 177 runs), **seven distinct jobs have been cancelled by the 6h `statement_timeout` that the `postgres` role carries via pg_db_role_setting**: jobid 22 rebuild-ec-incremental-mon (2 of 3 runs), 26 treemap-individuals-global-refresh, 17 donor-party-rollup-refresh, 24 donor-rollup-refresh (4 consecutive, 07-27..07-30), 13 financial-entity-totals-incremental (2 of 5), the retired jobid 1, and worst — **jobid 12 rule-taggers-weekly, which has failed 4 of its 5 retained runs at exactly 21601-21613s with nothing watching it at all**, i.e. the weekly industry tagger has essentially not completed since late June. **Two compounding harms.** (1) Each of these is silent: `cron.job_run_details` is the only place the failure is recorded, nothing reads it except the [[FIX-968]] `check_cron_job_health()` shipped today, and that detector deliberately treats blowouts as REPORT-ONLY (escalating on them would fail the canary most Tuesdays and train the alert to be ignored — the FIX-943 cause-vs-consequence rule). So they stay greppable but unalerted by design, pending this fix. (2) **A blowout does not fail fast — it runs flat out for six hours and then dies**, and on a 2-vCPU Pro Small that sustained load is the single largest cause of the `job startup timeout` class: pg_cron runs with `cron.use_background_workers=off`, so it opens a fresh libpq connection per firing with a ~10s window, and under a 6h squatter that window is blown. Measured directly — on 08-03 jobid 24 was starved by jobid 22 mid-blowout; on 08-04 jobids 13, 24 AND 12 were all starved by jobids 26+17 both mid-blowout. So the blowouts are self-amplifying: a job that cannot finish takes out the jobs scheduled after it. **Fix shape — ⚠ CORRECTED 2026-08-09:** the original shape below is necessary but NOT sufficient, and it names the wrong severity. **jobid 24 was ALREADY chunked ([[FIX-944]]) and still took the site down on 08-08** — so the harm that matters is **request-path saturation**, not job-vs-job starvation, and a fix that merely reschedules cron jobs so they stop colliding does nothing for it. Each arm needs a per-run cost/time budget that keeps a firing off the request path at all, not just a cursor that lets it resume. Original shape, still correct as far as it goes: chunk the remaining arms the way [[FIX-944]] and [[FIX-965]] chunked theirs (per-chunk COMMIT + a resume cursor in `pipeline_state`, so a 6h cancel keeps its progress instead of discarding it), starting with jobid 12 since it is the one that essentially never succeeds — but pair every chunking with a budget. **Scope note 2026-08-09:** jobid 24 has since been addressed by [[FIX-1002]]/[[FIX-1003]]; FIX-969 remains OPEN for jobids 12, 13, 17, 22 and 26. Note `SET statement_timeout` inside a procedure body does NOT re-arm the running timer (measured 2026-07-31, documented in donor-rollup-sweep.ts) — chunking is the only lever, not a timeout bump. Prerequisite input for the rollup-capacity work; the per-job p50/p95/max envelope table is already filed in the audit above so it need not be re-derived. **DATED OCCURRENCE — 2026-08-24, the THIRD PGRST002 outage (appended cc-prompt-76; append-only).** Prod ran degraded 08:00–13:16 UTC: 327 cron firings with 199 dead on `job startup timeout` (60.9 percent), PostgREST answering 503 on every sampled path, and `canceling authentication due to timeout` at 12:15:44. **The offender is named: jobid 22 `rebuild-ec-incremental-mon`**, whose donations window 5 ran as a single 3h49m statement ([[FIX-1101]]); the ~200 MB WAL burst at 11:21–11:28 and the 50.2 s checkpoint fsyncs belong to it. **The 57014s were NOT the site's own queries.** Craig expanded the 12:16:29 UTC log row: user `authenticator`, statement = PostgREST's schema-cache catalog load. `pg_roles` confirms `authenticator` carries `statement_timeout=8s` (with `lock_timeout=8s`; `anon` is 3s, `authenticated` 8s), so under I/O starvation the recursive catalog walk cannot complete, PostgREST fails its cache load, and every request becomes PGRST002 → 503. That is the [[FIX-1063]] mechanism for the third time — 08-18 treemap, 08-19 unwindowed EC, 08-24 windowed-but-skewed EC. **THE PATTERN ACROSS THE THREE, which is what this bullet's regime must inherit: each closure bounded ONE offender while the cliff itself stayed.** FIX-1002/1003 bounded jobid 24; [[FIX-1069]] bounded jobid 22's statement count; [[FIX-1071]] gave it an external budget — and the box still went unavailable, because nothing yet bounds the AGGREGATE concurrent write pressure, and nothing protects the `authenticator` schema-cache load from it. A per-job budget is necessary and demonstrably not sufficient. **Corollary measured the same morning: a starved job's budget is not a control at all** — both `donor-rollup-refresh` firings (09:00 and 12:00) died with `job startup timeout` and never ran, so FIX-1002's per-run budget never engaged, and `rule-taggers-daily` (06:30) died the same way. Cross-ref [[FIX-968]], [[FIX-944]], [[FIX-965]], [[FIX-951]], [[FIX-943]], [[FIX-950]], [[FIX-1002]], [[FIX-1003]], [[FIX-1063]], [[FIX-1072]], [[FIX-1101]], [[FIX-1102]]. **PARTIAL STRUCTURAL ANSWER FILED 2026-08-27 — [[FIX-1111]].** This bullet's corrected shape ("each arm needs a per-run cost/time budget that keeps a firing off the request path at all, not just a cursor that lets it resume") is now implemented for jobids 2/22 by replacing them with a paced crawl: one bankable unit per firing, a duration-based throttle sensor that backs off 2 h when the box slows, and blackout windows. Does NOT close this bullet — jobids 12, 13, 17 and 26 still have the shape described above, and the [[FIX-1112]] census establishes that three of them cannot even converge after a cancel because they write their watermark only at end-of-run. Fix order and the proposed schedule moves: docs/audits/2026-08-27-io-budget-burst-ledger.md sections 5 and 7. **RE-MEASURED 2026-09-20 (cc-139) — cc-139 EXPECTED to close this as superseded and THE MEASUREMENT REFUSED IT; the bullet stays OPEN.** Prod, read-only, full retained `cron.job_run_details` window 2026-06-29 08:00 → 2026-09-20 19:22 UTC (56,931 runs / 48 jobs — note retention is the entire history, not the ~30 days usually quoted). **WHAT IS GONE:** the 6 h ceiling this bullet is named for no longer exists — `pg_db_role_setting` has `postgres` at `statement_timeout=3h` (with `work_mem=256MB`) since [[FIX-1185]], and across ALL 41 scheduled jobs there were **ZERO** `canceling statement due to statement timeout` failures in the last 30 days. Of the four jobids the 2026-08-27 append left open: **12 `rule-taggers-weekly` is HEALTHY** — its last two runs COMPLETED at 4,692.4 s (09-08) and 4,837.7 s (09-15) against a 7,200 s budget, so the '4 of 5 retained runs at exactly 21601-21613 s' shape is refuted; **13 `financial-entity-totals-incremental`** is `active=f` and owned by [[FIX-1031]]; **17 `donor-party-rollup-refresh`** is owned by [[FIX-993]]. **WHY IT DID NOT CLOSE — two independent reasons.** (1) **jobid 26 `treemap-individuals-global-refresh` is NOT clean for 30 days**, which was cc-139's stated gate: 4 firings, 2 failed — 08-25 `job startup timeout` at 24.7 s, and 09-01 cancelled by the [[FIX-1063]] watchdog at 5,409.8 s against its 5,400 s budget — before 09-08 (1,084.2 s) and 09-15 (994.1 s) both succeeded. Its own shape has resolved (no statement-timeout blowout in 45 days, last two runs 5x inside budget) but the window is not clean. (2) **jobid 17 has ZERO successes in its last four firings** and is in a worse state than when this bullet was written: 08-25 `job startup timeout`, 09-01 watchdog cancel at 1,814.6 s, and BOTH 09-08 and 09-15 dead on `server restarted` with a NULL `end_time`. [[FIX-993]] owns the staleness but not the `server restarted` cause. **TO CLOSE:** a clean 30-day window on 26, and an explanation for 17's `server restarted` legs. jobid 17 donor-party-rollup-refresh vs the September restarts — REFUTED (cc-141 read 11; Craig's call 2026-09-21: leave the job ACTIVE and record the negative result). The Logs API for 2026-09-15 15:00-15:20 UTC, read before its 7-day window closed, shows job 17 starting at 15:00:00.011, a healthy box through 15:08:01.829 (time checkpoint writing 14 buffers / 0.0 percent; both 2-minute watchdogs completing in under 2 s), then 106 s of total log silence and a new postmaster at 15:09:48.281 reporting 'database system was interrupted; last known up at 2026-09-15 15:06:05 UTC'. Crash recovery yes — but NO 'out of memory', NO 'terminated by signal', NO 'server process was terminated', and no shutdown request: the postmaster vanished without logging anything, which is a container or host-level event, not a Postgres-side memory kill. The coincidence then dissolves on the census: prod restarted SEVEN times in the retained window — 08-18 (rule-taggers-weekly), 08-24 and 08-26 (entity-connection-stats-rebuild), 08-29 at 06:00 and 07:30 (ec-crawl, refresh-derived-mvs-daily), 09-08 and 09-15 (jobid 17) — spread across Tue/Mon/Wed/Sat, and jobid 17 was in flight for only TWO of them. Only LONG jobs are ever caught, which is exactly what random restart timing produces, because catch probability scales with time in flight. The one CONFIRMED resource failure, 09-01's 1,814.6 s watchdog kill on array_agg(DISTINCT fr.from_id), was already removed by [[FIX-950]] and is verifiably absent from the live procedure. SEPARATELY, and still open: jobid 17 has ZERO successes since 2026-07-28 (08-04 hit the 6h ceiling at 21,628.6 s; 08-11, 08-18 and 08-25 startup timeouts; 09-01 watchdog cancel; 09-08 and 09-15 server restarted) — that is its own bug, not a restart cause. jobid 26 treemap-individuals-global-refresh (Tue 14:00) is 2 of 4 over 30 days and SUCCEEDED on both restart days (09-15 994.1 s, 09-08 1084.2 s), finishing about 53 min before the 15:09:48 restart, so it is not implicated either. APPEND 2026-09-22 (cc-145, THE REFUTATION ABOVE DOES NOT SURVIVE A THIRD TUESDAY; jobid 17 is now filed as [[FIX-1212]]): the random-timing argument (two of seven restarts; only long jobs are ever caught) was sound on its inputs, and two new inputs overturn it. (1) 09-22 was the third consecutive jobid 17 firing to take the box down: every full-mode firing since 09-08, 3 of 3. (2) 09-22 did not restart, it degraded, and the degradation has a dose-response that a random restart cannot have. pg_cron failed every job with job startup timeout from 15:10:00, 10 min into jobid 17. It was mostly succeeding again by 18:18, 10 min after the FIX-1185 3h role ceiling cancelled jobid 17's stage build at 18:07:58. The 09-01 array_agg failure was removed as recorded above. What remains is a separate statement in the same procedure: the full/bootstrap branch's single GROUP BY over every donation at work_mem 256MB. The 08-04 run died in that statement too (CREATE TEMP TABLE dpr_stage, 21,628.6 s). jobid 26 treemap stays not implicated: it ended at 14:15:16 on 09-22, and the box was measurably healthy for about 45 min after it on all three days. The prompt that re-tested this assumed a ~14:30 death on 09-22, but the log is normal to 15:08:40. rule-taggers-weekly lost its 09-22 firing to the same outage (job startup timeout at 16:00:16). Audit: docs/audits/2026-09-22-tuesday-crash-read.md. APPEND 2026-09-23 (cc-146): jobid 17 is no longer one unbounded statement. [[FIX-1212]]'s windowed full rebuild reached Pro at 02:34:22 UTC: sixteen donor-id windows, each its own transaction, with a guard that stops before a window would take the CALL past 1,500 s, under the 1,800 s cron_job_budget. Its first prod success since 2026-07-28 has NOT happened: the supervised bootstrap is blocked on [[FIX-1213]] (session:claim-prod makes the claimant's own CALL skip). So jobid 17 is still without a success in the eight firings since 07-28 (FIX-1112's five from 08-04 to 09-01, then 09-08, 09-15 and 09-22), and Tue 09-29 15:00 will run the new windowed shape in full mode unless the bootstrap lands first. <!--id:FIX-969-->

- 🔴 L — **nightly fec_bulk donor-entities cannot finish inside the 150-min GHA cap — cycle 2026 never completes** — Since 2026-08-03 the nightly-sync `fec-phase` job has run 1h45m-4h every night and **cycle 2026 has never completed**. Diagnosed 2026-08-08 while investigating unexpected prod write load. **The loop:** FEC published `indiv2026.zip` Sun 2026-08-02 15:45 UTC; [[FIX-903]]'s weekday drop-probe (`packages/data/src/pipelines/fec-bulk/drop-check.ts`) sees a file newer than `pipeline_state.fec_indiv_watermark` and fires `fec_bulk` on NON-Sunday nights (the Sunday gate in `weekly-gate.ts` is intact and not involved) — **but the FIX-903 probe is only ONE of the two triggers; see correction (3) below**; the stage grinds through part of an 840,338-row donor-entity upsert, then **the job is guillotined by the 150-minute GHA `timeout-minutes` cap**; the [[FIX-754]] checkpoint survives, so the next night resumes and repeats. **Prod evidence 2026-08-08 05:00 UTC:** `fec_bulk_run_state` = cycle 2026, donor-entities cursor 484,000/840,338, `started_at 2026-08-06T06:42:47Z` (same run-state alive 2 days), `fec_last_modified "Sun, 02 Aug 2026 15:45:35 GMT"`; `fec_indiv_watermark` 2026 = `"Sun, 26 Jul 2026 15:18:36 GMT"` (a week behind). **Run history:** Jul 31 10m OK, Aug 1 10m OK, Aug 2 (Sun) 1h44m OK, Aug 3 4h03m cancelled, Aug 4 1h54m fail, Aug 5 1h44m fail, Aug 6 4h04m cancelled, Aug 7 1h44m fail, Aug 8 cancelled manually at 1h36m. **Why it can never converge:** FEC republishes `indiv{yy}.zip` every Sunday ~15:20 UTC; when the new file lands the resume identity (`fec_last_modified`/etag) stops matching and `resolveResumePoint` resets the cursor to 0 — the stage cannot finish inside one nightly budget, so the weekly republish always laps it. **Collateral:** a 20s `pg_stat_statements` delta during the run showed trivial request volume (0.05-0.30 calls/sec, no bot) but badly degraded latency — proposals-list RPC 1,568ms avg, officials-list 841ms, a scalar p_id RPC 4,625ms; plus autovacuum on `financial_entities` running 26+ min and two `data_sync_log` rows stuck `running`. **Fix directions (not yet chosen):** cut the donor-entity stage's wall-clock (or give it a bigger budget) so it fits inside the 150-min cap instead of being guillotined by it; make resume survive a same-cycle republish rather than resetting to 0; and/or move the indiv stage off the nightly onto its own dispatch-only workflow with a larger budget (cf. the [[FIX-740]] USASpending precedent). **⚠ CORRECTED 2026-08-09 — three factual errors in the original bullet, re-measured across all six nights' GHA logs and run history.** **(1) It never OOM'd.** No `FATAL ERROR`, no `Reached heap limit`, no `Ineffective mark-compacts` appears in ANY of the six nights' logs. The dominant mechanism is the **150-minute GHA `timeout-minutes` cap** — 08-03 and 08-06 both log `exceeded the maximum execution time of 2h30m0s` — plus two UNRELATED prod statement timeouts (08-04 congress-officials; 08-05 `loadExistingCandidateNames`). 08-07's `The hosted runner lost communication with the server` log blob returns `BlobNotFound` against an empty step conclusion: one night of six, mechanism unknown. **`NODE_OPTIONS=--max-old-space-size=12288` was never load-bearing**, so 'cap the heap' is not a fix direction. **(2) 'It has never once completed' is false.** On 08-04 fec_bulk finished **cycle 2022** in full and logged `⟳ Cleared fec_bulk_run_state — cycle 2022 complete (FIX-754)` — the 08-03 → 08-04 resume chain converged. Cycle 2026 is the stuck one and only started 08-06. **(3) The trigger was BOTH mechanisms, not [[FIX-903]] alone:** 08-03/04/08 fired via the [[FIX-754]] resume state, 08-05/06 via the FIX-903 drop probe. `pipelines/index.ts` evaluates the probe ONLY when `fecResumeState === null`, so the two branches substitute for each other and neither can be blamed in isolation. Pairs with [[FIX-996]] (checkpoint write path); [[FIX-997]] may be partly downstream of this. <!--id:FIX-995-->
- 🟠 M — **Three pg_cron jobs failing with 'job startup timeout'** — Noticed while investigating [[FIX-995]]. `cron.job_run_details` on prod shows a recurring `job startup timeout` failure — the job never starts, so it is a pg_cron background-worker slot/startup problem, not a statement-level timeout inside the procedure: jobid 29 `abuse-events-retention` 2026-08-08 04:15 failed 28s; jobid 11 `rule-taggers-daily` 2026-08-07 06:30 failed 29s; jobid 28 `contract-flow-rollups-refresh` 2026-08-06 14:00 failed 30s; jobid 9 `refresh-derived-mvs-daily` 2026-08-06 06:00 failed 10s; jobid 11 `rule-taggers-daily` 2026-08-06 06:30 failed 10s. These are intermittent — the same jobs succeed on other days (`abuse-events-retention` succeeded 08-07 and 08-06; `refresh-derived-mvs-daily` succeeded 08-07 in 1h07m). The ~10-30s time-to-failure is consistent with pg_cron giving up waiting for a background worker slot. **Check whether the failures correlate with the nightly FEC window ([[FIX-995]]) holding workers/connections** — `refresh-derived-mvs-daily` at 06:00 and `rule-taggers-daily` at 06:30 both sit inside the window where the fec-phase job is mid-run, and `max_worker_processes` on the Pro Micro is small; if so, several may clear once [[FIX-995]] is fixed and the real item here is alerting. **No retry or alerting exists on pg_cron failures today** — these were only found by manually reading `cron.job_run_details`. **CLOSED 2026-09-20 (cc-139) — superseded.** Re-measured read-only on prod over the full retained `cron.job_run_details` window (2026-06-29 08:00 → 2026-09-20 19:22 UTC, 56,931 runs across 48 jobs). All three legs of this bullet are answered elsewhere. (1) **The alerting ask is BUILT** — [[FIX-1073]]'s `startup_timeout_tiers` is an ESCALATING key in `check_cron_job_health()` (migration `20260903000100_fix1073_startup_timeout_tiers.sql`; N=6 consecutive startup timeouts for one job, or M=10 in a 60-minute bucket across all jobs), consumed by `canary-check.ts`, with the bare `startup_timeouts` list deliberately demoted to REPORT-ONLY. This bullet's closing sentence — 'No retry or alerting exists on pg_cron failures today' — is refuted for the alerting half; the retry half is [[FIX-1137]]. (2) **The FIX-995 correlation this bullet asked to check is settled** — [[FIX-995]] closed 2026-09-13, and the 08-06/08 instances sat in its nightly window. (3) **The failure CLASS belongs to [[FIX-1194]]**, widened by cc-130 to 'any sufficiently long job starves the forker' and given its counterfactual by cc-132. The four named jobs over the last 30 days: `abuse-events-retention` 1 startup timeout of 30 firings, `rule-taggers-daily` 3 of 30, `contract-flow-rollups-refresh` 0 of 4, `refresh-derived-mvs-daily` 0 of 30 (its single failure was `server restarted`). Nothing here is separately buildable. Residual lives in [[FIX-1194]] (the class) and [[FIX-1137]] (recovering an abandoned firing). <!--id:FIX-997-->
- 🟡 S — **financial_entities carries 310 MB of never-scanned indexes that every non-HOT update still maintains** — Found in the [[FIX-1008]] write-surface census. A non-HOT UPDATE inserts a new entry into EVERY index on the table, whether or not that index's columns changed — so an index nothing reads is still paid for on every write. `pg_stat_user_indexes` on prod 2026-08-09 (counters cumulative, `stats_reset` NULL, so this is the full post-cutover window) shows five `financial_entities` indexes with **idx_scan = 0**: `financial_entities_donated_positive` 145 MB, `financial_entities_recipient_count_idx` 70 MB, `financial_entities_individual_state_idx` 61 MB, `financial_entities_canonical_name_type` 19 MB, `financial_entities_canonical` 15 MB — **310 MB across 5 of 19 indexes**, never once scanned. The donor stage does 840,338 upserts per cycle and ~73% of its updates are non-HOT, so this is real write amplification on the pipeline's most expensive stage, and it competes for a 256 MB `shared_buffers` that the working set already dwarfs. `financial_entities_individual_state_idx` deserves particular attention: it is an EXPRESSION index over `(metadata->>'state')`, and `metadata` is rewritten by the donor upsert on every conflict, so it re-evaluates the expression on every non-HOT write and it is a HOT blocker in its own right. Do NOT batch-drop these on the strength of idx_scan alone — verify each against the read paths first, because a zero-scan index can still be (a) a uniqueness constraint, (b) an ON CONFLICT arbiter, or (c) serving a route that has simply not run since the counters started. None of these five is unique or an arbiter, which is what makes them candidates. Note the contrast on the other table: `financial_relationships_usaspending_unique` is also idx_scan = 0 at 485 MB but MUST stay — it is the usaspending pipeline's conflict arbiter, and that pipeline is manual-dispatch-only since [[FIX-740]]. Cross-ref [[FIX-1008]], [[FIX-884]]. <!--id:FIX-1012-->
- 🟠 M — **FIXES.md is both hand-edited and machine-edited — derive status from done.log instead of flipping checkboxes in place** — Filed 2026-08-09 out of the cc-prompt-55 trunk-guard work; this is the real fix, of which the guard is only the detector. **The class.** docs/FIXES.md carries two kinds of content under one writer contract: bullet prose, which only Craig edits by hand, and the leading checkbox, which fixes:sync flips in place. An in-place machine edit of a hand-edited file is the entire concurrent-write class — syncFixesMd reads the whole file, maps every line, and writes the whole file back, so any hand edit that lands inside that window is silently reverted with no conflict, no error, and a diff that looks intentional. docs/done.log does NOT have the problem, and the reason is structural rather than lucky: it is append-only, and appends from two sessions merge where in-place edits do not. **Proposal.** Make done.log the single source of truth for status: FIXES.md holds prose and the id marker only, and fixes:check COMPUTES the checked set from the log rather than reading it back out of the markdown. Then FIXES.md is only ever hand-edited, done.log is only ever appended, and the class disappears rather than being detected. **Two known bugs die with it.** (1) fixes:sync silently no-ops its checkbox flips on a CRLF working copy — it normalizes CRLF on read but the bullet regex still has to match, and a mismatch is indistinguishable from nothing-to-do. (2) The state-in-two-places reconciliation that produced checkboxesFlipped: 2 against four closures on 2026-08-09; with status derived there is nothing to reconcile. **Cost, honestly.** A one-time migration of the current checkbox state into the log for any bullet whose [x] has no completion row, plus updating every reader of [x] — fixes-clean.mjs (which selects source bullets by checkbox), fixes-archive.mjs, fixes-housekeep.mjs and session-check.mjs. Do NOT change done.log format as part of this; the append-only contract is what makes the whole thing work. **Relationship to the guard.** scripts/lib/trunk-guard.mjs now aborts any of these scripts when origin/main or HEAD moves between their read and their write, which converts the silent revert into a loud refusal — necessary, and strictly weaker than removing the in-place write. Keep the guard afterwards regardless: fix:add still appends to FIXES.md and fixes-archive still rewrites it. Cross-ref [[FIX-461]], [[FIX-462]], [[FIX-361]], [[FIX-314]], [[FIX-369]], [[FIX-1015]]. <!--id:FIX-1016-->
- 🟠 S — **FIX-1024 re-filed: financial-entity-totals-incremental IS enabled on prod — it has failed its last 3 firings, and 2 of them are collateral from the weekly derived-MV refresh** — **[[FIX-1024]]'s premise is contradicted on prod — same symptom, different bug.** That bullet says jobid 13 `financial-entity-totals-incremental` was created paused and never enabled, so `financial_entities.total_donated_cents` refreshes monthly rather than weekly. It was measured on the local prod clone and the bullet correctly warned to verify against prod first. **Verified on prod 2026-08-13: `SELECT jobid, jobname, active FROM cron.job WHERE jobid=13` returns `active = t`.** The job IS enabled and IS firing on schedule. The stale aggregate is real, but the cause is not a missing enable — **the job is enabled and failing.** `cron.job_run_details` for jobid 13, newest first: runid 233 `2026-08-11 09:00` **failed** after 5m25s, `job startup timeout`; runid 167 `2026-08-04 09:00` **failed** after 10s, `job startup timeout`; runid 115 `2026-07-28 09:00` **failed** after **6h00m11s** with `ERROR: canceling statement due to statement timeout` in `UPDATE public.financial_entities fe SET total_donated_cents = agg.total FROM (SELECT fr.from_id, SUM(fr.amount_ce...`; runid 71 `2026-07-21 09:00` **succeeded** in 7m04s. So the last success was 2026-07-21 and there have been three consecutive failures since — the columns have been effectively monthly-refreshed for three weeks, which is FIX-1024's observed consequence reached by a completely different route. **The two failure modes are distinct and only one is about this job.** (a) runid 115 is a genuine cost problem: the incremental UPDATE ran the full 6h role budget and was cancelled, which means the 'incremental' path is not actually bounded and wants the [[FIX-1002]]/[[FIX-1018]] treatment (dirty-set + wall-clock budget + per-chunk COMMIT) that the donor rollup already has. (b) runids 233 and 167 are `job startup timeout` — pg_cron could not launch a background worker at all, so the procedure never ran a single statement. **That is almost certainly collateral, not this job's fault.** jobid 13 is scheduled `0 9 * * 2` and jobid 10 `refresh-derived-mvs-weekly` is `0 7 * * 2` — the same Tuesday, two hours earlier. On 2026-08-11 the 07:00 weekly stalled on `chord_donor_state_party_flows_mv`, and the SAME `job startup timeout` message hit jobid 25 at 07:30 and jobid 26 at 08:15 before postgres went log-dark at 08:29 (see the weekly-refresh restart FIX). jobid 13's 09:00 failure that morning sits squarely inside that window. **So do not 'fix' jobid 13 by tuning jobid 13.** Order of work: (1) resolve the weekly derived-MV refresh first — while it can wedge the box every Tuesday morning, every job scheduled behind it is unreliable and its failures are unattributable; (2) then re-measure jobid 13 on a healthy Tuesday to see whether runid 115's 6h overrun reproduces; (3) only then decide whether the incremental UPDATE needs bounding. **Also worth noting for the audit trail:** FIX-1024 says nothing ever enabled the incremental job, but prod disagrees, so something DID enable it outside the migration record — worth finding, since an unrecorded prod cron mutation is its own hazard. Correct FIX-1024's clone-measured claim rather than acting on it. Cross-ref [[FIX-1024]], [[FIX-1009]], [[FIX-702]], [[FIX-726]], [[FIX-736]], [[FIX-1002]], [[FIX-1018]], [[FIX-968]], [[FIX-977]].  **RE-MEASURED 2026-08-26 (cc-prompt-89) on the healthy Tuesday this bullet asked for — and the answer is worse than "needs bounding": the job can no longer succeed, by construction.** Step (2) of this bullet said to re-measure jobid 13 once the weekly derived-MV refresh stopped wedging Tuesday mornings. Done: **runid 15453, 2026-08-25 10:00:00 → 12:14:07 UTC, 8,047.8s, cancelled `due to user request`** by the [[FIX-1063]] watchdog inside `financial_entity_recipient_count_rebuild`'s UPDATE, after writing 775,098 financial_entities rows. **TWO OF THIS BULLET'S PREMISES ARE REFUTED.** (1) *"It wants the [[FIX-1002]]/[[FIX-1018]] treatment (dirty-set + wall-clock budget + per-chunk COMMIT)"* — **it already HAS two of the three.** `refresh_financial_entity_totals_incremental` builds a watermark-driven dirty set, chunks it 500 ids at a time, wraps each chunk in `BEGIN ... EXCEPTION WHEN OTHERS`, and COMMITs per chunk. The only missing leg is the **wall-clock budget**, which is precisely why an EXTERNAL watchdog had to stop it. Narrow the prescription accordingly. (2) *"The columns have been effectively monthly-refreshed"* — **no.** total_donated_cents and recipient_count were written for **775,098 entities on 08-25** (609,357 rows in the 10:00 hour, 165,741 in the 11:00). The job does real work every Tuesday; it just never FINISHES. **THE ACTUAL DEFECT IS A RATCHET.** `pipeline_state.financial_entity_totals_watermark` reads **2026-08-07 04:41:54**, last written **2026-08-08 02:34:35**. The procedure advances the watermark only at the very end and only when `v_failures` is empty — but **plpgsql's `WHEN OTHERS` deliberately does not catch QUERY_CANCELED**, so a watchdog cancel aborts the whole procedure instead of being recorded as a chunk failure, and the watermark write is never reached. The per-chunk COMMITs preserve the DATA but there is no resume cursor, so they preserve no PROGRESS. Measured consequence: every firing since 08-08 rebuilds a dirty set of everything since 2026-08-07, which today is **4,654,818 financial_relationships rows and growing**, and the dirty-set build alone — `count(DISTINCT from_id)` over that slice — **blew a 120s statement timeout on an otherwise idle box** while this was being written. That is why runid 4361 (08-18, 5,539s) died inside `SELECT array_agg(DISTINCT fr.to_id) ...`, i.e. in the dirty-set build itself, never reaching a single UPDATE. **Each failure makes the next firing strictly more expensive, which guarantees the next failure.** Expected value is now negative: it cannot complete, and each attempt spends the box's entire daily I/O budget. **CORRECTED CONSEQUENCE:** this job does not merely fail. Per [[FIX-1107]], its 08-25 firing pinned prod's disk at the 3,000 IOPS cap for ~75 minutes and left the box at <150 IOPS / 60–75 percent IOwait until roughly 18:00 — starving jobids 24, 25, 26 and 17 into `job startup timeout` and cancelling seven GitHub Actions runs. **ACTION TAKEN 2026-08-26, Craig-authorized — jobid 13 PAUSED on prod.** `SELECT cron.alter_job(job_id := (SELECT jobid FROM cron.job WHERE jobname = 'financial-entity-totals-incremental'), active := false)`, resolved by NAME via `scripts/fix1031-pause-fe-totals-cron.mjs` (guarded to refuse unless exactly one job matches; supports --dry-run). cron.job jobid 13 `0 10 * * 2` went **active = t → f**. **Deliberately NOT unscheduled** — cron.job_run_details is the only instrument this job has. **BLAST RADIUS, and it is not nothing.** Both columns freeze at their current values, which were last materially advanced 08-25 11:46:30 and only for the ~775k entities the run reached — so the columns are now **internally inconsistent, part-fresh and part-2026-08-07**, which is its own argument against leaving the job limping. Readers of `total_donated_cents`: the donor page (`apps/civitics/app/donors/[id]/page.tsx`, including its OG/meta description), browse and typeahead ranking (`api/browse/execute.ts`, `api/browse/typeahead/route.ts`), `api/search/entity/route.ts`, `api/graph/snapshot/route.ts`, `api/graph/treemap/route.ts`, and the `treemap_officials_by_donations` RPC. Readers of `recipient_count`: `api/graph/connections/route.ts` (node sizing), `api/graph/individual-donors/route.ts` (connector-donor sort order), plus `rebuild_entity_search_index` and `search_graph_entities`. The monthly `financial-entity-totals-reconcile` (jobid 14, `0 12 1 * *`) and `reconcile_recipient_count` remain **active**, so orphan zeroing continues and the drift is one-directional — values go stale, not wrong-by-orphan. **UN-PAUSE CONDITION — TWO things, not one.** (a) The [[FIX-969]] regime: per-run wall-clock budget plus a resume cursor so a cancel banks progress, the [[FIX-1002]]/[[FIX-1021]] shape — and its cancel handler must name QUERY_CANCELED explicitly, because `WHEN OTHERS` will not catch it. (b) **A watermark reset or bootstrap pass**, because the frozen 2026-08-07 watermark is now itself the cost driver; re-enabling with (a) but without (b) reproduces the 08-25 starvation on the very next Tuesday. FILE, DO NOT BUILD. Cross-ref [[FIX-1107]], [[FIX-969]], [[FIX-1063]], [[FIX-1002]], [[FIX-1021]]. **CLOSED 2026-09-20 (cc-139) — superseded; BOTH un-pause conditions this bullet set are now satisfied, which is what it was waiting on.** Verified read-only on prod. **(a) The [[FIX-969]] regime was built — as the fe-crawl, not as a repair to jobid 13.** jobid 46 `fe-crawl` (`run_fe_totals_crawl`, `*/30`) fired **905 times in the last 30 days with 899 successes** and 6 `job startup timeout`s, p95 327.1 s against a 1,800 s budget, and it carries the peer-backoff gate that stands it down when its sibling crawl is throttled. **(b) THE RATCHET IS BROKEN — this is the decisive reading.** `pipeline_state.financial_entity_totals_watermark` now reads `last_indexed_at = 2026-09-19 22:42:57+00`, written 2026-09-20 00:00:01+00 — against the frozen **2026-08-07 04:41:54** this bullet measured as the cost driver that 'guarantees the next failure'. The crawl has walked the watermark to yesterday, so the dirty-set-of-everything-since-08-07 that blew a 120 s statement timeout on an idle box no longer exists. jobid 13 itself remains `active=f`, last fired 2026-08-25 (8,047.8 s, watchdog cancel), with its 7,200 s budget row still in place. The monthly `financial-entity-totals-reconcile` (jobid 14, `0 12 1 * *`) fired 2026-09-01 12:00 and **succeeded in 134.2 s**, so orphan zeroing continues one-directionally exactly as this bullet predicted. Whether jobid 13 is ever re-enabled, now that fe-crawl does the work continuously, is a decision rather than a defect. Residual lives in [[FIX-969]] and [[FIX-1194]]. <!--id:FIX-1031-->
- 🔴 M — **Under Attack mode is load-bearing cost control (~$21/day) holding a crawler that has not backed off — it must not come off before the limiter works, and setting CIVITICS_APP_URL to civitics.com would kill both monitoring jobs instantly** — **Found by the 2026-08-15 traffic/cost-spike triage (docs/audits/2026-08-15-traffic-cost-spike.md). REVISED 2026-08-16 — the original bullet claimed UA mode was 'inverted' and not working; that was wrong and is retracted in full. Craig authorized this edit.** **What the original got wrong and why:** it dated UA mode to docs/CLOUDFLARE.md's 2026-08-08 posture row (Craig actually enabled it **2026-08-15 22:00 UTC**), and it read a 4-row `vercel logs` sample — capped and sampled, a shape sample and never a volume measure — as evidence the crawl was unaffected. Both errors pointed the same wrong way. **What is actually true, measured via the new `node scripts/cf-analytics.mjs --hours 24`:** the crawl began **2026-08-15 06:00 UTC** and ran machine-flat at **7,163-7,562 requests/hour for 16 hours**, essentially all on www.civitics.com, walking distinct entity UUIDs across /officials, /donors, /proposals, /jurisdictions, /institutions — the exact [[FIX-637]]/[[FIX-683]] crawl shape, every request a cold origin render (zone cache hit rate ~0.1 pct). **Under Attack mode then worked as a step function at exactly the enablement hour: 21:00 UTC = 7,313 x HTTP 200 / 0 x 403; 22:00 UTC = 445 x 200 / 6,850 x 403; 23:00 UTC = 28 x 200 / 7,268 x 403. Origin-reaching traffic fell ~99 pct.** **The cost this is holding back.** Because billing day 15 contained only ONE crawl hour, the triage's first cost pass understated the exposure ~11x. Rebuilt from Cloudflare request counts: ~\$1.23e-4 per origin-reaching crawl request, so ~\$21/day / **~\$640/month** unmitigated, against a ~\$31/month run-rate. **So UA mode is currently the single largest cost control on the platform, and it is temporary scaffolding, not a fix.** **The crawler has NOT backed off** — still 7,033-7,336 req/hour, now absorbed by Cloudflare instead of Vercel. Turn UA mode off before [[FIX-1038]] lands and the burn resumes within the hour. **~1 pct still leaks to origin** (28-86 x 200/hour, independently matching ~1 req/min in `vercel logs`), and nothing throttles that residual because the limiter is fail-open. NB the original bullet inferred a **challenge-solving / headless crawler** from a 403-vs-200 comparison plus a Speed Insights step; **both legs are withdrawn** — a ~1 pct leak is ordinary challenge bypass, not a JS-capable client, and the \$0.65 Speed Insights line was a fixed once-per-cycle charge (Vercel billing cycle is Aug 14 - Sep 14), not usage. **THE LIVE LANDMINE — unchanged by the revision, and now MORE urgent because UA mode is staying on.** .github/workflows/platform-snapshot.yml is the ONLY workflow that calls the app hostname (verified by grep; nightly.yml, sync-canary-check.yml, rebuild-entity-connections.yml and audit.yml do NOT), and both its jobs use secrets.CIVITICS_APP_URL with a fallback to the .vercel.app host. They are green today ONLY because that secret is unset or points at .vercel.app. docs/CLOUDFLARE.md section 5 states Cloudflare 403s every scripted probe to civitics.com including browser-UA curl, and this session measured exactly that. **The moment anyone sets CIVITICS_APP_URL to https://civitics.com, the platform snapshot AND the [[FIX-1026]] request-path probe both die** — and per FIX-1026's own header a timed-out probe reports as 'cancelled', which GitHub does not notify on. That blinds the platform during the next incident. Pin the fallback, or document the constraint at the secret. **THE CRAWLER IS IDENTIFIED — a targeted rule is now possible.** Cloudflare security events (user-supplied, ~30k mitigated) name it: **`meta-webindexer/1.1`**, UA `Mozilla/5.0 (Windows NT 10.0; Win64; x64) AppleWebKit/537.36 ... (compatible; meta-webindexer/1.1 (+https://developers.facebook.com/docs/sharing/webmasters/crawler))`, **ASN 32934 (Facebook, Inc.)**, IPv6 2a03:2880:f800:35::, Host www.civitics.com, mitigated by Rule ID `iuam` (i.e. Under Attack mode itself, not the WAF rule). A single named UA on a single ASN is the easy case: a **targeted WAF rule on that UA or ASN would let UA mode come off** without giving the crawler back the whole site — that is the cheapest exit and it should be evaluated before the general exit plan below. **What it is walking:** `/graph?entity=<uuid>` — **44,689 of 147,611 requests in 24h (~30 pct), the single largest path by a wide margin**, the rest a long tail of distinct entity UUIDs. Two consequences, both filed as [[FIX-1042]]: `/graph` gets **NO rate-limit bucket at all** in middleware.ts, and robots.txt's `Disallow: /*?` ([[FIX-513]]) did not stop it. **Exit plan — what must be true before UA mode goes off:** (a) the rate limiter working again ([[FIX-1038]]) **and** `/graph` actually bucketed ([[FIX-1042]]), since 30 pct of this crawl targets a route the limiter never inspects; (b) the 'Common Exploit Paths' WAF custom rule verified — **now PARTIALLY validated by this incident**: the same 24h window shows `/klex.php`, `/file.php` and `/index/function.php` scanner probes all returning **403**, so the rule is live and doing its job on the traffic class docs/CLOUDFLARE.md section 2 credits it with; section 2's real test (watch Vercel Fluid CPU after UA mode goes off) still stands. **Also promotes two docs/CLOUDFLARE.md Pending checkboxes into tracked work:** 'Turn Under Attack mode off' and 'Add the FIX-799 Skip rule'. The FIX-799 skip rule is DEPRIORITISED by this triage — H3 (RSC amplification) was killed on evidence (invocations per edge request 1.70 vs a 1.63 median; fluid CPU per invocation FELL 41 pct), so it is a soft-navigation UX fix, not a cost fix. **Separable exposure, filed here for context, NOT the incident cause:** the .vercel.app origin answers 200 with no protection at all. H2 was KILLED — the only 8 of 100 log rows on that hostname were our own cron and probe — so this is defence-in-depth, ranked last. If it is ever done, note that the request-path-probe job sends NO auth header and would break under any Host restriction, unlike the snapshot job which carries CRON_SECRET. Cross-ref [[FIX-513]], [[FIX-637]], [[FIX-683]], [[FIX-797]], [[FIX-799]], [[FIX-1026]], [[FIX-1038]], [[FIX-1040]]. <!--id:FIX-1039-->

Candidates worth separating before proposing a fix: checkpoint or WAL pressure, the 256MB shared_buffers cache-churn baseline, Supabase-side platform activity invisible to us, and the TLS-handshake deaths [[FIX-1072]] section 6 relocated the problem to. Cross-ref [[FIX-1103]] which measured the SAME class on 08-24 and found the request-path probe blind to it, [[FIX-1072]], [[FIX-1066]], [[FIX-1063]], [[FIX-1031]].  **WRITER ATTRIBUTED 2026-08-26 (cc-prompt-89) — the opening burst is jobid 13, the unattributed core is a depleted I/O balance refilling, and the tail is a second writer.** Craig's Supabase Observability chart for 2026-08-25 shows disk IOPS pinned at the **3,000 provisioned cap from 10:00 to ~11:15 UTC**, then throttled to **<150 IOPS at 60–75 percent IOwait** through ~13:00, then ~0 IOPS with the CPU sampler dropping samples 13:00–15:00. cron.job schedules `financial-entity-totals-incremental` (jobid 13) at `0 10 * * 2`. The catalog confirms it end to end. **cron.job_run_details runid 15453: 2026-08-25 10:00:00.075 → 12:14:07.904 UTC, 8,047.8s, failed, `ERROR: canceling statement due to user request`** inside `UPDATE public.financial_entities fe SET recipient_count = agg.cnt FROM (SELECT fr.from_id, COUNT(DISTINCT fr.to_id)...`. **It did NOT reach the 6h statement_timeout** — it was cancelled at 2h14m by the [[FIX-1063]] budget watchdog (`user request`, not `statement timeout`), so this bullet's and [[FIX-1031]]'s expectation of a 16:00 death is refuted. **financial_entities write volume by hour on 08-25: 10:00 → 609,357 rows; 11:00 → 165,741 rows; 12:00 → ZERO**, with max(updated_at) at **11:46:30.849**. The final 28 minutes (11:46:30 → 12:14:07) committed nothing — one 500-entity chunk ran 28 minutes and was killed. Per-chunk cost went ~3s (10:00 hour) → ~8s (11:00 hour) → **>1,680s**, and the inflection sits exactly at the ~11:15 throttle. The burst did not merely coincide with the throttle; **the throttle is what turned a 3-second chunk into a 28-minute one.** Nothing else ran: between jobid 24 donor-rollup-refresh succeeding at 09:08:29 (509.6s) and jobid 12 starting at 16:00, the only firings are the 11:05–11:18 vacuum jobs at <=7.8s each, and no data_sync_log row spans the gap. **VERDICT — jobid 13 owns 10:00–12:14, but the day has THREE segments and only the first is its process.** (1) **10:00–12:14, jobid 13, sole sustained writer.** Watchdog failures **0/60 in the 10:00 hour** — the burst itself is free, the box is spending credit it holds — then **35/60 (58 percent) at 11:00** once throttled. (2) **12:14–16:00, nobody, and the box gets WORSE**: 43/60 (72 percent) at 12:00, 50/60 (83 percent) at 13:00, **58/60 (97 percent) at 14:00**, peaking with nothing scheduled; seven platform-snapshot GHA runs cancelled 11:36→17:28 after ~5 min each while every run before 11:36 and after 17:52 finished under a minute. **This is the segment this bullet filed as NOT ATTRIBUTED, and it is now attributable — not to a process but to a spent burst-I/O balance refilling. Killing the writer does not return the credit it spent.** (3) **16:00–18:02, jobid 12 rule-taggers-weekly**, itself cancelled at 7,325.8s by the same watchdog — a SECOND sustained writer landing while the box was still in deficit; recovery to 2/60 (3 percent) arrives at 18:00, within two minutes of it ending. **THE OPERATIONAL FACT — the I/O budget, not CPU, is the scarce resource, and it is small.** Three measured bursts now exist: **08-24 ~30 min** above baseline (post-FEC-replay), **08-25 ~75 min** at the 3,000 cap, **08-26 ~70 min** at ~2,500. Provisioned baseline is **1,000 IOPS / 22 MB/s**; the disk cap is **3,000**. So the box holds roughly **30–75 minutes per day of above-baseline I/O**, and once spent it runs at **~150 IOPS / 60–75 percent IOwait for hours** while the balance refills. Every bulk writer must fit that envelope or it does not merely fail — it spends the whole day's credit and starves every job behind it. **The contrast that proves this is a budget and not a load problem:** on 08-26 donor-rollup-refresh ran 09:00–10:59 AND 12:00–13:59 (7,171.9s and 7,186.6s, both `partial — budget exhausted`) — four hours of sustained work — and jobids 40/44 recorded **5/60 failures across the entire day**. Sustained load inside the budget is harmless; a burst that exhausts it is not. Cross-ref [[FIX-1031]] (the writer, now paused), [[FIX-969]] (the regime it needs), [[FIX-1101]] (the 08-26 EC run that stayed inside the envelope). **STRUCTURAL ANSWER FILED 2026-08-27 — [[FIX-1111]].** This bullet's operational fact (the I/O budget, not CPU, is the scarce resource, and it is small) is now the premise of a shipped design: the EC rebuild runs as a background CRAWL, one bounded unit per firing at */15, paced so aggregate spend stays under the refill rate rather than bursting through the daily pool in 75 minutes. The per-firing ceiling is 96 x 346 s = 60 percent of the day's refill and cannot be exceeded by any cycle shape. Does NOT close this bullet: the crawl bounds ONE writer, and the aggregate-concurrent-write-pressure gap this bullet names is untouched. The 12:14-16:00 hole remains unattributed to any process. Burst ledger and the slack map: docs/audits/2026-08-27-io-budget-burst-ledger.md. **CLOSED 2026-09-20 (cc-139) — redirected to [[FIX-1194]], which now owns by name the class this bullet filed as unattributed.** The gate cc-139 set for closing was: *is there any hour since 2026-09-01 with >=10 of 30 watchdog fork failures OUTSIDE both a landing and a long job?* Measured on prod across both `*/2` watchdogs, **seven hours clear the bar and every one is attributable.** **2026-09-01 12:00** (11/30 on each watchdog) and **13:00** (18/30 on each) ran under THREE concurrent long writers — `donor-rollup-refresh` 12:00:00→13:52:27 (6,747.3 s), `donor-rollup-orphan-sweep` 12:30→13:26 (3,393.2 s) and `ec-crawl` 12:45→13:20 (2,113.7 s, itself watchdog-cancelled at 13:16). **2026-09-07 20:00** (12/30, 13/30) and **21:00** (10/30, 10/30) are the [[FIX-1165]] set-2 apply, whose platform-scoped tail was cancelled by hand 28 minutes in for 202 front-door cancellations; the corrective commit landed 20:56 UTC and the FIX-1165 filing at 21:38 UTC, and `ec-crawl`/`fe-crawl` firings are themselves missing from that window while `front_door_watch` — which runs from `vercel_cron` and forks no backend — kept reporting `ok`. **2026-09-17 04:00, 05:00 and 15:00** are set 1's landing and drain, already written up on [[FIX-1194]]. So the aggregate-concurrent-write-pressure gap this bullet named is not a separate unattributed class: **the 09-01 instance IS the aggregate case**, and cc-130 already widened [[FIX-1194]] past 'a landing's derived EC work' to 'any sufficiently long job starves the forker', which cc-132 sharpened to 'long SINGLE STATEMENTS holding a backend, not rewrite volume as such'. The 2026-08-25 12:14-16:00 hole stays attributed as this bullet's own 08-26 append left it — a spent burst-I/O balance refilling, not a process — and nothing in this re-measurement disturbs that. Residual lives in [[FIX-1194]]. <!--id:FIX-1107-->
- 🟠 M — **ec-crawl free-running 6h cooldown walks its cycle onto the fixed 06:00 derived-MVs job — keep the crawl out of that window** — The 2026-08-29 prod seizure (about 75 minutes, unclean restart 07:21:57, manual restart 07:40:57) had ec-crawl mid-stats-arm when refresh-derived-mvs-daily fired. Evidence: cron.job_run_details shows ec-crawl starting 06:00:00.096 and refresh-derived-mvs-daily starting 06:00:00.118, 22ms apart, both later marked failed with server restarted; the ec_crawl recent_units ring shows the crawl was executing entity_connection_stats_windows window 3 of 16 at that moment, and that unit was only completed later at 18:46:33 after Craig re-enabled the job. The timing mechanism is a free-running clock meeting a fixed one: min_cycle_interval_minutes is 360, the previous cycle closed 2026-08-28 23:00:43, so the cooldown expired 05:00:43, the 05:00 firing skipped with reason cycle_cooldown and the 05:15 firing opened a cycle 45 minutes ahead of the 06:00 job. Thu 08-27 and Fri 08-28 were clean for exactly this reason and not because the workload was smaller: their cycles opened 06:30 and 19:00, so the 06:00 ec-crawl firings took 14.9s and 0.03s. So the collision is not a rare alignment, it recurs whenever a cycle happens to close near 23:00-00:00 UTC. Compounding: rebuild_entity_connections_contracts sits in ec_arm_source_fingerprints disabled_arms, so it runs un-gated every cycle (330.4s on 08-29, 344.9s on 08-28), rewrites entity_connections, and therefore re-dirties the entity_connection_stats_windows fingerprint every cycle, which is why the expensive 16-window stats arm recurs on every cycle rather than only when edges genuinely changed. That loop is worth its own look. Interim data-leg APPLIED to prod 2026-08-29 19:03 UTC with Craig approval: ec_crawl blackout set to one window from 05:45 to 07:00 UTC, covering refresh-derived-mvs-daily (06:00, 12 to 13 minutes recently) and rule-taggers-daily (06:30), skipping 5 firings. Mechanism proven on LOCAL first: a covering window returns run false with reason blackout, a non-covering window stays clear, wrap-around works, a malformed entry warns and is ignored so it fails OPEN, and the driver records the skip only in pipeline_state ec_crawl skips blackout with NO data_sync_log row, which is the deliberate logging asymmetry. Read it back that way, not from the sync log. Durable options: make the blackout permanent policy, or give the crawl a peer signal on the derived-MVs writer in the fe-crawl peer-backoff shape so it yields to what is actually running instead of to a hardcoded clock; prefer the peer signal, since a blackout is a clock guarding against a clock. The blackout removes the co-tenant, NOT the cause: refresh-derived-mvs-daily still runs and still holds the memory, see [[FIX-1123]]. Cross-ref [[FIX-1111]] [[FIX-1117]] [[FIX-1107]] [[FIX-1103]] [[FIX-589]]. **RE-MEASURED 2026-09-20 (cc-139) — the blackout is holding and has been WIDENED; the durable option this bullet actually prefers is still NOT built.** Read-only on prod. **The clock leg works.** `pipeline_state.ec_crawl.blackout` is `[{from 05:45, to 09:00}]` — wider than the 05:45-07:00 window applied on 2026-08-29, now covering `refresh-derived-mvs-daily` (06:00) and `rule-taggers-daily` (06:30) with real margin. Since 2026-09-01 there are **seven** `entity_connections_rebuild` rows inside 05:45-09:00 and **all seven are `status='skipped'`** — zero cycles opened in the window, so the 06:00 collision this bullet filed has not recurred. Lifetime skip counters: `blackout` 267, `cycle_cooldown` 1,098, `backoff` 170. **THE PEER SIGNAL EXISTS BUT POINTS AT THE WRONG THING.** The fe-crawl peer-backoff shape this bullet asked to copy is real and firing — `fe_crawl` carries 59 `peer_backoff` skips and writes 'peer crawl ec_crawl is backed off until … — the box is throttled for both of us' — but its implementation (`20260828020000_fix1031_969_fe_totals_crawl.sql`, the `p_peer_key` branch) reads a SIBLING CRAWL's `pipeline_state.backoff_until`. It is crawl-to-crawl only. `ec_crawl_gate`'s body mentions neither `peer` nor `blackout`, and no function in the gate family references the derived-MVs writer at all. So the crawl still yields to a hardcoded clock rather than to what is actually running — precisely the substitution this bullet argued against ('a blackout is a clock guarding against a clock'). Open on the durable ask, not on the interim mitigation. <!--id:FIX-1124-->

The watchdog only ran again at 07:26:00, after the crash restart, and did then correctly reap the row. Required shape to evaluate: a memory-class signal that does not need a NEW backend in order to act, meaning an in-process check inside the long-running unit itself or a bounded resource ceiling set before the expensive statement rather than a canceller racing it from outside; plus, as the cheap outside backstop, a cron_job_budget row for refresh-derived-mvs-daily, which today has none. See [[FIX-969]] for the full census. APPEND 2026-09-22 (cc-142): UNCHANGED by P2-A, stated so nobody assumes otherwise. - 🟠 M — **89 public functions carry a decorative statement_timeout proconfig that cannot bound them** — Surfaced by the FIX-1123 class census. A FUNCTION-level SET statement_timeout proconfig NEVER bounds that function's own execution: the timer is armed once when the top-level statement begins and changing the GUC mid-statement does not re-arm it. The value IS applied (current_setting inside the body reads it) which is exactly why the pattern looks like it works. Verified on this PG 17 local: CREATE FUNCTION _t() RETURNS int LANGUAGE plpgsql SET statement_timeout = '1s' AS 'BEGIN PERFORM pg_sleep(3); RETURN 1; END'; SET statement_timeout='60s'; SELECT _t(); returns 1 and does NOT error, while current_setting('statement_timeout') inside the body reads 1s. This is the same PG 17 behaviour [[FIX-703]] recorded for procedures, never generalised to functions. THIS IS NOT THEORETICAL: rebuild_entity_search_index carries statement_timeout=1200s and ran 21,638 s on 2026-08-31, ended only by the postgres role's statement_timeout=6h, taking prod's REST API down from 06:11 to the manual reset at 23:01 ([[FIX-1123]]). 89 public functions carry the pattern. Triage by gap between believed and real bound: the heavy writers are the dangerous ones because the real ceiling is the role's 6h -- rebuild_entity_connections (believed 60min), rebuild_entity_connections_contracts (30min), rebuild_financial_entity_donation_totals / _full / _received_totals (30min each), rebuild_entity_connections_appointments / _external / _votes / _votes_full (15min), rebuild_entity_search_index (1200s), refresh_jurisdiction_page_cache and refresh_official_content_ids (600s). The request-path ones (get_official_page 5s, get_agency_page 3s, the 2s trigger helpers) are mostly benign because the authenticator role's statement_timeout=8s is a REAL bound that arms at statement start and is tighter than most of them -- but any request-path function whose proconfig claims MORE than 8s (get_financial_entity_naics 120s, get_group_donor_totals 30s, get_official_donor_rollup_full 300s, chord_contract_flows_full 300s, get_top_connected_officials 60s, get_quality_counts 60s, get_proposal_counts_by_agency 60s) is claiming a bound it does not have and is silently getting 8s instead, which is a DIFFERENT bug in the opposite direction. Fixes to evaluate: (a) for pg_cron-driven writers, an EXTERNAL watchdog is the only thing that works -- FIX-1030's per-unit watchdog shape -- but see [[FIX-1125]], it must be able to fork; (b) for callers that CAN re-arm, issue SET LOCAL statement_timeout as its own statement BEFORE the call so the next statement arms from it; (c) at minimum relabel every inert proconfig so nobody reads it as a guard again. Cross-ref [[FIX-703]] [[FIX-1123]] [[FIX-1125]] [[FIX-1030]]. HALF 1 LANDED 2026-09-13 (cc-124), no Fixes: trailer — stripping alone makes no bound real. CENSUS: 91 not 89, clone and prod byte-identical, all prokind=f — A 24 (request path), B 21 (service_role RPC), C1 15 (called from a COMMITting procedure), C2 5 (single-transaction procedure), D 10 (direct-pg script), E 16 (trigger/helper/inner); docs/audits/2026-09-13-fix1128-proconfig-census.tsv. EXPERIMENTS (docs/audits/2026-09-13-fix1128-experiments.md): (1) proconfig inert, 3s sleep completed under 1s; (2) NO SUCH OBJECT — a routine with any SET clause cannot COMMIT (invalid transaction termination), which is why the census is 91 functions and zero procedures; (3) THE C1 FIX IS REFUTED — set_config(...,false) inside a procedure survives the COMMIT and arms nothing, the 3s sleep completed; (4) the LOCAL form reverts at COMMIT and also arms nothing; (5) FIX-703's instant-re-timeout did NOT reproduce and the truth is worse — after a CAUGHT 57014 the timer is disarmed for the rest of the CALL, 14.0s of work completed under a 4s bound; (6) pg_cron DOES run a multi-statement command, cancelled at 1.0046s, and it ran on the CLONE (scheduler live: 38 jobs, 405 runs/24h) so the sanctioned prod cron probe was not needed. ALSO MEASURED: a proconfig cannot LOOSEN either (2s session, 30s proconfig, cancelled at 2.001s, identical to no proconfig), so the strip is behaviour-neutral in both directions. STRIP: supabase/migrations/20260913000000_fix1128_reset_inert_statement_timeout.sql, applied local and prod, 91 to 0 on both, remaining-proconfig md5 identical 91/91 0 changed. GUARD: pnpm check:proconfig (scripts/check-proconfig-timeouts.mjs, 18 fixture assertions, seventh blocking check in tests.yml), baselined at that migration because history carries the pattern 183 times. BOUNDS (docs/audits/2026-09-13-fix1128-bounds.tsv): class B's real bound MEASURED at 8s (7s OK, 9s/10s/10s cancelled 57014 at ~8.1s); 29 within, 4 EXCEEDS real, 58 unobserved; pgss does NOT record cancelled statements, so every 'within' is silent about cancels. Filed [[FIX-1180]] (get_official_page completes above the anon 3s bound), [[FIX-1181]] (two service_role RPCs COMPLETE past the measured 8s bound), [[FIX-1179]] (three census functions have no caller anywhere). _fix1128_probe_sleep is live on prod and local, service_role only, and Half 2 DROPs it. HALF 2 (cc-125) SCOPE IS NOT WHAT THE DESIGN ASSUMED: the per-unit C1 re-arm is dead (experiment 3), so the only mechanism that bounds a pg_cron procedure is the job's command string (SET statement_timeout='...'; CALL ...), which bounds the WHOLE CALL, not a unit; a per-unit budget needs the procedure to time itself against a budget and stop, the cron_job_budget shape ([[FIX-1063]] [[FIX-1125]]); and whatever it does must handle experiment 5 — one caught cancel unbounds every unit after it. Half 2 closes this FIX. HALF 2 (cc-125, 2026-09-14) — THE MECHANISM IS REFUTED AND THIS FIX DOES NOT CLOSE. (i) The 697/703 claim that statement_timeout re-arms after a COMMIT was an INFERENCE, never a measurement, and is false on PG 17 (cc-124 case 3). (ii) Worse, the replacement is false too: SET statement_timeout = 'N'; CALL public.proc() in a pg_cron command string does not bound the six census procedures, it stops them running — a multi-statement pg_cron command executes in an IMPLICIT TRANSACTION BLOCK and a procedure cannot COMMIT inside one, so the run dies at its first COMMIT about 10 ms in with 'invalid transaction termination' and zero units executed. Not the SET: SELECT 1; CALL proc fails identically while the bare CALL succeeds. Four cases plus two transaction-boundary escapes on the clone's live scheduler, docs/audits/2026-09-14-fix1128-half2-census.md section 4; the cc-124 experiments audit carries a correction note. (iii) H2 needed no work — all three C1 procedures already record-and-STOP on a cancel (FIX-1021/1028/1056/1111/1112, and refresh_derived_mvs's own comment already states experiment 5's finding), and the three C2 procedures have no EXCEPTION block at all so a cancel rolls their single transaction back whole; prod proof is ec-crawl runid 26047. (iv) The census is TEN jobs not 8-9 (ec-crawl is the extra) and nine already carried a cron_job_budget row; the gap was rule-taggers-daily, not refresh-derived-mvs-daily as [[FIX-1125]] recorded, and it now has one at 7200 s. - 🟠 M — **pg_cron startup-timeout option A: re-fire an idempotent job once** — FIX-1073 shipped option B (tier the signal so a lone abandoned firing reports rather than pages; see the FIX-1073 migration for the derivation of N=6 consecutive / M=10-per-hour from 30 days of cron.job_run_details). Option A is the other half and is deliberately UNBUILT: when a firing is abandoned at startup, pg_cron has no queue and no retry, so the work simply does not happen until the next scheduled firing — for donor-rollup-refresh that is 12 hours, for a weekly rollup a week. Measured on prod 2026-09-02: 1,849 startup timeouts across 16 days, and three weekly rollups sat multi-week stale largely because their once-a-week firing kept landing in a bad minute. Option A re-fires such a job ONCE, shortly after the abandoned firing, but ONLY for jobs that are safe to re-enter. That exclusion list is the actual work and is why this is filed rather than done: a job is eligible only if it is idempotent or watermark-resumable AND cheap enough that a double-run cannot itself saturate the box. The vacuum-analyze jobs and the watermark-driven crawl arms are the obvious yes; anything holding a session advisory lock, anything that TRUNCATEs, and anything already near the 6h statement_timeout are the obvious no. Needs: an eligibility column or declaration table, a re-fire mechanism that cannot itself loop, and a cap on re-fires per window. Cross-refs the rollup_watch_overrides declaration table added by FIX-1059 — that is the natural home for a per-pipeline eligibility flag.  **SECOND SOURCE OF SKIPPED FIRINGS, added 2026-09-11 by [[FIX-950]].** Twenty guarded pg_cron procedures and the nightly's writer phases now stand down while a supervised prod session is held, writing `status='skipped'` with `metadata->>'skip_reason'` in one spelling across both mechanisms — `prod session held: <reason>` — so this re-fire can key on `skip_reason LIKE 'prod session held%'` without caring whether the firing was pg_cron's or GitHub's. Note these are NOT startup timeouts and want a different eligibility answer: the work did not fail, it deferred, and the operator who held the box may well have re-run it by hand. FIX-950 deliberately built no re-fire of its own, per D8.**RATE RE-MEASURED 2026-09-20 (cc-139) — the motivation has weakened sharply without going to zero, and what is left argues for a NARROWER Option A than this bullet describes.** Prod `cron.job_run_details`: **239 `job startup timeout`s across the 16 days 2026-09-04 19:26 → 2026-09-20 19:24 UTC**, against the **1,849 in 16 days to 2026-09-02** that motivated this filing — an **87 percent** reduction. The distribution matters more than the total: **twelve of those sixteen days recorded exactly ZERO**, and the 239 sit almost entirely in four — 09-07 (63, the [[FIX-1165]] set-2 apply), 09-08 (2), 09-14 (16) and 09-17 (158, the [[FIX-1187]] set-1 landing). By UTC hour over 30 days the class concentrates at 12:00 (7.83 percent of firings), 06:00 (6.19), 15:00 (5.89) and 13:00 (5.88), and is 0.00 at 00:00, 03:00 and 22:00. **So the class is now EPISODIC and landing-correlated rather than chronic weather**, which is [[FIX-1194]]'s reading arrived at from the other direction. **Consequence for this bullet's design, and it is a real one:** a job that loses a firing now loses it almost exclusively *during a supervised landing*, when an operator is present and an automatic re-fire is most likely to collide with the very work that starved it. The eligibility table this bullet calls 'the actual work' therefore wants a third test beside idempotency and cost — *not while `prod_session_state()` is held* — which also composes with the [[FIX-950]] `skip_reason LIKE 'prod session held%'` source the 2026-09-11 append added. Still design-first; still unbuilt. APPEND 2026-09-22 (cc-142, note only — no design change): a recovered-firing design can now read `cron_job_budget_action.acted_via` as well. FIX-1194 P2-A added the column ('pg_cron' | 'vercel') and a second, non-pg_cron firing path for both */2 watchdogs, so "did this tick's work actually happen?" is answerable for the watchdogs without inferring it from the absence of a failure row — a 'vercel' row inside a startup-timeout window IS the recovered firing, already recorded. That does not make this bullet's option A (re-fire an idempotent job once) unnecessary: P2-A covers exactly two jobs, the two whose work is cheap, idempotent and safe to double-fire, and says nothing about the other thirty-odd. It does mean the re-fire design has a worked precedent for the idempotency argument (the ledger's `runid` PK and the unit watchdog's `watchdog_canceled_at` guard make a double firing a no-op by construction) and a rendered surface to measure against (receipts §9). APPEND 2026-09-22 (cc-145, note only): the 09-22 outage shows the limit of option A. Every pg_cron job failed with job startup timeout from 15:10:00 to 18:08, and about one in three failed from then until the 22:28 restart. rule-taggers-weekly (16:00), both crawls and all seven 17:0x vacuums lost their firings. A single re-fire minutes later would have landed inside the same outage. Option A is for transient startup timeouts (the Tue 13:00 class). It does not remedy a box that cannot fork; preventing that is [[FIX-1212]]. <!--id:FIX-1137-->
- 🔴 M — **Three weekly rollups have not succeeded since late July (surfaced by the FIX-1011 census widening)** — Found by widening the freshness census from 13 pg_cron-sourced pipelines to all 49 (FIX-1011). Three weekly pg_cron rollups have failed every firing for four-to-five weeks. Read from cron.job_run_details on prod 2026-09-02: (1) jobid 17 donor-party-rollup-refresh, last success 2026-07-28, then statement timeout 08-04, startup timeout 08-11 / 08-18 / 08-25, user-request cancel 09-01 — data_sync_log last complete 2026-07-28, 879h stale; (2) jobid 25 agency-staffing-rollup-refresh, last success 2026-08-04, then startup timeout on all four subsequent firings 08-11 / 08-18 / 08-25 / 09-01 — 712h stale; (3) jobid 26 treemap-individuals-global-refresh, last success 2026-07-28, then statement timeout, startup timeout, user-request cancel, startup timeout, user-request cancel — 669h stale. Two distinct causes are mixed here and both need addressing. The startup timeouts are the connection-accept pressure FIX-1073 now tiers, and a weekly job is maximally exposed to it because one bad minute costs a whole week — this is the strongest argument for FIX-1137 (option A re-fire). The statement timeouts and user-request cancels are the jobs themselves being too heavy or being axed by the cancel-handler, which is a separate question per job. All three render user-visible surfaces: donor_party_rollup_mv backs the party-flow chord, agency_staffing_rollup_refresh backs agency FTE panels, treemap_individuals_global_refresh backs the donor treemap. The canary escalates on all three as of FIX-1135/1011 so they will stay visible, but escalation is not remediation. Needs: a per-job decision on cost/chunking, then a catch-up run for each off-peak.
