- 🟠 S — **remediate-fec-emit-residue's dry run prints no tail-step cost-class table — the one script of the four that skips FIX-1165(c)** — FIX-1165(c) is explicit: 'The dry run must print each tail step's cost class -- manifest-scoped, platform-scoped-with-owner, or platform-scoped-orphan -- so the trade is visible before the go-ahead rather than discovered at minute 28.' Three of the four fr-rewrite landing scripts do it: DrainOptions.printTable's own comment says 'The three remediation scripts already print it at their dry-run planning stage -- before the go-ahead, which is the point of it (FIX-1165 c) -- so they pass false to avoid a second copy at execution time.' remediate-fec-emit-residue.ts is the fourth and does neither. It never calls printTailTable itself, and the only call it inherits is the default-true one inside drainFrRewrite, which is reached only on the --apply path -- so the table prints AFTER the delete has committed, which is exactly the moment it is no longer decision-useful. Observed on prod 2026-09-14 in cc-119: the dry run against the 128-recipient manifest printed the manifest header, the keyed re-check (match 128, shrunk 0, grown 0, gone 0) and 'TO DELETE: 67,933 rows', then stopped. Seventeen declared tail steps, three manifest-scoped and fourteen platform-owned, and not one of them named. FIX SHAPE, one call: import declareDrainTail plus printTailTable and print them in the dry-run branch before the 'DRY RUN -- nothing written' line, then pass printTable:false to drainFrRewrite the way the siblings do so the apply path does not print it twice. The structural test that reads the scripts' source should gain a case asserting all four print the table before their go-ahead point, since three-of-four passing is what let this ship. Cross-ref [[FIX-1165]], [[FIX-1074]], [[FIX-1106]]. FOLLOW-UP (cc-134, 4306bc45): the table this FIX brought to the fourth script now carries six columns, not four. schedule and guarded are read live from cron.job at print time via readOwnerSchedules(), and the owner strings lost their hard-coded schedule parentheticals in the same change - the structural test's literal moved to printTailTable(declareRemediationTail(defer), defer, owners) and gained a second assertion that readOwnerSchedules(client) precedes it, because dropping the third argument compiles fine and silently renders ? in both columns. <!--id:FIX-1183-->
- 🟠 S — **fec_emit_runs leaks a never-completed row per nightly per incomplete cycle-source, and Guard 1 keys on the newest row** — complete_at is stamped only at the FIX-754 run_state clear (index.ts:2518), i.e. at CYCLE completion. A nightly that opens an emit run for a cycle-source it does not finish leaves the row open forever, and the next nightly opens another. Prod 2026-09-14 shows three such rows already, from two nights: 96260b92 (2024/fec_bulk_pac, started 2026-09-12T22:47:37Z), 5697a031 (2026/fec_bulk_pac, 2026-09-12T22:48:48Z) and b09496d6 (2024/fec_bulk_pac, 2026-09-13T22:53:52Z), all keys=0, all complete_at NULL. WHY IT IS NOT COSMETIC: audit-fec-emit-residue.ts Guard 1 takes the newest run for the slice by started_at -- deliberately NOT the newest COMPLETE one, because anti-joining an older set would delete rows this week's run legitimately re-emitted -- and refuses when that row is incomplete, with no --force. So one orphan row makes the audit permanently unrunnable for its slice. (2024, fec_bulk_pac) is in that state now and cannot be audited at all. (2026, fec_bulk_pac) was in it on 09-13 and only escaped because the 09-14 run happened to complete; the same night's 2024 arm did not. The guard is right and the leak is what is wrong. FIX SHAPE, and the choice matters: either close the run row on the same exit paths that abandon the cycle -- a terminal status distinct from complete, so Guard 1 can tell 'abandoned' from 'still running' and refuse only on the latter -- or stop opening a run row until the cycle actually starts emitting. The first is better because an abandoned run's partial key set is still evidence about what got emitted before the abandonment. Either way Guard 1's predicate should become 'newest row that is complete OR terminally abandoned', never 'newest row'. Cross-ref [[FIX-1106]], [[FIX-754]], [[FIX-995]], [[FIX-957]]. **SHIPPED 2026-09-14 (cc-126) IN A DIFFERENT SHAPE — the premise above is wrong in the direction that matters, and the read is what changed it.** This bullet assumes the orphan rows are PARTIAL and proposes a terminal 'abandoned' status so the guard can tell abandoned from running. **They are not partial: each of the three holds a FULL key set — 135,192 / 111,729 / 135,192 — and the two cycle-2024 rows from consecutive nightlies hold byte-identical counts.** The keys column reads 0 only because it is written at the stamp. Reading the branch rather than inferring: the pas2 arm reaches openEmitSink for every cycle in CYCLES on every fec phase — it sits AHEAD of the FIX-193 indiv watermark short-circuit and has no gate of its own — while stampEmitRunsComplete stamps cycleSinks, filtered to the ONE cycle whose indiv work completed. The indiv arms cannot produce this leak on the FIX-754 skip path, because 'complete in prior run — skipping' takes a branch in which openEmitSink is never evaluated, so no row is opened at all. SO THE FIX IS TWO PARTS, NOT A STATUS COLUMN. **(B1)** the pas2 arm stamps ITSELF, per cycle, at the end of its own stage — admissible where the indiv arms' single stamp is not, because it is one un-cursored batch with no resume and FIX-686's loud-abort contract THROWS rather than returning short, so there is no prefix it could stamp; cycleSinks now excludes fec_bulk_pac. **(B2)** superseded_at / superseded_by (migration 20260914000100, pushed to prod 2026-09-14): at the next begin() for a slice, an older incomplete row is superseded, its TRUE key count written into keys from the pre-delete snapshot, and its keys deleted — one statement, and the run_id inequality leaves a resumed run alone. Guard 1 now walks newest-first: complete -> use it; superseded with keys=0 -> skip; superseded with keys>0 -> REFUSE naming it (a prefix WAS written, which is the whole reason the guard is 'newest' and not 'newest complete'); neither -> REFUSE as before. Closure is Pattern B — the receipt is the next scheduled nightly showing every pre-existing orphan superseded with zero live keys and the night's pac rows stamped per cycle. cc-141 could NOT confirm the supersession on prod, and the bullet stays open. Both fec_emit_runs and fec_emit_keys are UNLOGGED (pg_class.relpersistence = u), so PostgreSQL TRUNCATED them during the 2026-09-15 15:09:48 UTC crash recovery (see [[FIX-969]]). The three 09-12/13 open pac rows named above (96260b92, 5697a031, b09496d6) and the 09-14 complete indiv run 84470d3b were destroyed then, NOT superseded: fec_emit_runs holds 6 rows, every one started 09-19 or later, with superseded_at set on NONE and open rows = 0. The drop night therefore had nothing older to supersede, and the FIX-1184 path in emit-set.ts:119-140 has still never been exercised against prod. A zero open-row count is the right state but is not evidence for the mechanism.  APPEND 2026-09-22 (cc-144, READ ONLY — the leak shape did NOT recur): read the ledger after Monday's weekday nightly (fec phase 23:44:54-23:50:01 UTC on 09-21) and fec_emit_runs holds exactly the six rows cc-141 saw, every one with complete_at set and superseded_at NULL. Monday opened NO row at all — not an incomplete one. The reason is that begin() in emit-set.ts is what INSERTs the row, and a weekday nightly whose fec phase finds no new bulk inputs never reaches it, so there is nothing to leak. So the leak needs a nightly that STARTS a slice and does not finish it, which a no-input weekday run cannot do. Also worth recording: the (2024, fec_bulk_pac) orphan this bullet names as 'what it is today' is GONE — both the 09-19 and 09-20 2024 pac rows are complete — so the supersession logic already on trunk is doing its job. Guard 1 consequently refused nothing across all three cc-144 audits. <!--id:FIX-1184-->
- 🟠 M — **No in-backend per-job bound exists for a COMMITting pg_cron procedure — pick the replacement for the command-string route** — cc-125 measured FIX-1128 Half 2's plan and it is unbuildable. SET statement_timeout = 'N'; CALL public.proc() in a pg_cron command string does NOT bound any of the six census procedures — it stops them running: a pg_cron command with more than one statement executes in an IMPLICIT TRANSACTION BLOCK, and a procedure cannot COMMIT inside one, so the run dies at its first COMMIT about 10 ms in with 'invalid transaction termination' and ZERO units executed. It is not the SET: SELECT 1; CALL proc fails identically, while the same CALL alone succeeds. Four cases on the clone's live scheduler plus two transaction-boundary escapes that also fail, in docs/audits/2026-09-14-fix1128-half2-census.md section 4. Same rule as cc-124's experiment 2 (atomic context implies no COMMIT) on a different surface; cc-124 experiment 6 measured a SELECT of a non-COMMITting SQL function and the generalisation to PROCEDUREs was never re-measured. SO THE FIX-1063 cron_job_budget WATCHDOG IS THE ONLY PER-JOB BOUND THERE IS, and [[FIX-1125]] is the standing objection to relying on it alone: it must fork to act, and it is starved by exactly the condition it exists to end. Measured on prod, all 11 rows of cron_job_budget_action: the watchdog fires late by 4.8 s to 973.9 s past budget on a */2 (120 s) cadence, and SIX of the eleven were more than one full cadence late (286.9 / 315.9 / 973.9 / 846.9 / 176.4 / 125.1 s) — so the effective bound is budget plus an unbounded tail, not budget. Unwind after the cancel signal adds a median 12 s and once 249.4 s. THREE OPTIONS TO EVALUATE, none built: (a) dblink per firing — a single-statement command calling a function that opens one connection and issues SET then CALL as SEPARATE statements, which works because the two are separate round trips; costs one extra backend per firing, which is the FIX-1125 starvation class but ONE backend per job rather than one per unit, and dblink is available-but-not-installed on both databases so it is a security-surface decision; (b) a per-job role carrying the bound as a role-level GUC — REFUSED TODAY, cron.alter_job(username) needs superuser and postgres is not superuser on Supabase (pg_user.usesuper = f, local and prod), so it needs Supabase support or is dead; (c) lower the global ceiling, ALTER ROLE postgres IN DATABASE postgres SET statement_timeout, today 6 h — the ONE in-backend mechanism proven to bound a whole CALL across COMMITs (it is what ended FIX-1123's 21,638 s run), and the census separates cleanly because every healthy run fits under 7,125.6 s while every incident starts at 12,546.6 s, so a 3 h ceiling would cut every incident and no healthy run; the cost is that it applies to every postgres-role statement including migrations and interactive sessions. Supersedes the general command-string sweep Half 2 planned to file. Cross-ref [[FIX-1128]] [[FIX-1125]] [[FIX-1063]] [[FIX-1123]] [[FIX-1030]]. cc-127 RAN THE PHASE 0 CENSUS AND THE D3 GATE FIRED — no migration was written and nothing was pushed to prod (docs/audits/2026-09-14-fix1185-ceiling.md). THE MECHANISM IS PROVEN on the clone's live pg_cron scheduler: a role-level default cancelled unit 3 of a COMMITting CALL at 5.000 s across two intervening COMMITs, the record-and-STOP handler ran, the terminal row was written, the CALL RETURNED so pg_cron logged succeeded, and work_mem=256MB survived the ALTER; the 6h is project-set at supabase/migrations/20260701000000_fix703_pgcron_donations_per_window_commit.sql:415 (raised from the 90min at 20260630000000_fix697_pgcron_role_timeout.sql:36), every one of prod's 38 cron jobs runs as postgres, and check:proconfig already passes a role-level SET with no fixture needed. WHAT FAILED IS THE NUMBER. pg_cron writes succeeded for a C1 procedure that caught a cancel and returned — refresh-derived-mvs-daily ran 21,932.5 s on 2026-08-31 and logged succeeded while its own row says partial — so cron status cannot separate healthy from incident. Classified instead by the procedure's own data_sync_log row, FIVE runs above 2.5 h in the retained window logged complete: entity-connection-stats-rebuild 18,568.4 s and 14,932.7 s, rule-taggers-daily 14,028.3 s, refresh-derived-mvs-daily 12,546.6 s, donor-rollup-refresh 11,280.8 s — each launched by a bare single-statement CALL with no session bound of its own, and the design had classified two of them as contention incidents. WORSE, A 10,800 s CEILING INVERTS THE WATCHDOG: it would fire 3,600 s before donor-rollup-refresh's ACTIVE 14,400 s cron_job_budget and would tie exactly with contract-flow-rollups-refresh's ACTIVE 10,800 s, and since the watchdog is late by 4.8-973.9 s the ceiling would win every time — so for two active jobs the global timer becomes the operative bound and the per-job budget stops mattering, the opposite of the design's stated posture. Both jobs are absent from the design's census table, as is the fact that entity-connection-stats-rebuild has NO cron_job_budget row at all. In the trailing 30 days the longest clean run is 7,201.7 s, so 3h survives a 30-day basis but not the retained window; the next prompt must state which window the number is sized against, settle the watchdog inversion, and give entity-connection-stats-rebuild a budget row. **RE-DECIDED and SHIPPED to prod 2026-09-16 03:24 UTC (cc-128).** The gate that fired in cc-127 was about the WINDOW, not the mechanism, so the number is now sized on the stated trailing 30 days (2026-08-17..2026-09-16), read from each procedure's own data_sync_log terminal row: nothing in that window completes above 4,837.7 s. Three statements, one migration: the postgres role ceiling 6h to 3h in database postgres; donor-rollup-refresh 14400 to 9000 and contract-flow-rollups-refresh 10800 to 9000, so the ceiling stays the backstop instead of demoting the watchdog for two ACTIVE jobs; and a first budget row for entity-connection-stats-rebuild at 9000. Ordering holds by construction: 10800 minus the re-derived 30-day max watchdog lateness 963.8 s = 9836.2, above the largest active budget. work_mem=256MB survived. Two caveats recorded in docs/audits/2026-09-16-fix1187-shared-id-census.md section 6: donor-rollup's own note carries a 9,388 s pre-loop observation the new budget does not cover (it predates the window and the job is resumable), and the two INACTIVE rebuild-ec-incremental rows keep an 18,000 s budget now ABOVE the ceiling. **Receipt still pending** — contract-flow-rollups-refresh next fires Thu 2026-09-17 14:00 UTC, so cc-129 carries the record commit. cc-129 receipt status: the first daily after the 03:24 push is IN and clean (refresh_derived_mvs 2026-09-16 06:00 UTC, complete, 13 units, skipped_units [], unit_failures 0, budget_hit false, 335.9s against a 3,300s budget), and both donor-rollup-refresh firings logged complete under 9,000s at the FIX-983 horizon with no statement-timeout message on any job. The Thursday leg is still missing: contract-flow-rollups-refresh is 0 14 * * 4 and had not fired, so the record commit does NOT land in cc-129 either and carries to the next prompt. cc-130 read the receipt a third time and it is STILL not clean, so no record commit rode this run either. Thursday's contract-flow-rollups-refresh 2026-09-17 14:00 UTC is finally in and genuinely clean on both instruments (pg_cron succeeded 5,892.2 s; the procedure's own row complete 5,891.2 s, 500 recipients / 1,492 agency sectors) — well under both the 9,000 s budget and the 3 h ceiling, though about 3x the 1,900-2,000 s expected. But the FIRST DAILY did not complete: refresh_derived_mvs 09-17 06:00 was cancelled by derived-mvs-unit-watchdog at 06:20:06 on rebuild_entity_search_index after 1,018.2 s against a 900 s per-unit budget, units_ok 8 of 13, budget_hit false, four units never run. pg_cron recorded that same run as succeeded — the divergence is exactly why both instruments are read. Two true readings: the per-unit watchdog WORKED, which is evidence for the design; and the record's claim that the first daily completed under these bounds would be false. Next clean chance is the 09-18 06:00 UTC daily. cc-131 (2026-09-18): STILL not recordable, and this time purely on the clock. The record needs the 2026-09-18 refresh-derived-mvs-daily clean on both instruments; it fires at 06:00 UTC and the session ran 01:14-04:15 UTC, so it had not fired. The most recent daily is 09-17 and it is partial in exactly cc-130's shape: pg_cron says succeeded while data_sync_log records the watchdog cancelling rebuild_entity_search_index at 1,018.2 s against a 900 s unit budget, 8 of 13 units OK, 4 skipped, budget_hit false. That unit ran 236.5 s on 09-16 and 216.5 s on 09-15, so the 4.3x blow-up is set 1's churn and the cancel is the watchdog doing its job - but the record's claim needs a clean 13/13. Everything else holds: the donor-rollup firings since 09-16 are all succeeded and far under 9,000 s (max 1,774.1 s), and Thursday's contract-flow refresh is clean at 5,892.2 s. CLOSED 2026-09-19 (cc-132, the prod receipt cc-131 could not earn): both halves of the replacement are live on prod and were READ rather than assumed. (1) The in-backend ceiling: pg_db_role_setting carries {statement_timeout=3h,work_mem=256MB} for postgres-in-postgres and a prod backend reports current_setting('statement_timeout') = 3h. An instrument note worth keeping, because it nearly produced a false alarm here — pg_roles.rolconfig for postgres shows ONLY search_path, since a database-scoped role setting does not appear there; reading rolconfig alone would say the ceiling had been removed, which is exactly the RESET failure mode CLAUDE.md warns about and would have been wrong. pg_db_role_setting is the instrument. (2) The per-job arm: cron_job_budget holds 17 rows whose largest ACTIVE budget is 9,000 s (contract-flow-rollups-refresh, donor-rollup-refresh); the two 18,000 s rows belong to rebuild-ec-incremental and rebuild-ec-incremental-mon, both active=false, so an unfiltered max(budget_seconds) misreads this too. 9,000 sits under the 9,836.2 s that 3h minus the 963.8 s maximum watchdog lateness permits, so no live job silently demotes its watchdog to the global timer. THE RECEIPT: refresh-derived-mvs-daily 2026-09-19 06:00 ran 467.7 s succeeded on cron.job_run_details and complete on its own data_sync_log row with all 13 units in unit_seconds, skipped_units [], unit_failures 0, budget_hit false, slowest_unit_seconds 241.8 against a 900 s unit budget and 3,300 s cycle budget. The 09-18 daily is the same shape (479.0 s / 478.9 s, complete 13/13) so this is two consecutive clean dailies, against 09-17 which was partial at 1,207.6 s with 9 units run and 4 skipped. Zero statement-timeout messages on ANY pg_cron job since 2026-09-17, and every donor-rollup-refresh and contract-flow-rollups-refresh firing since 09-17 14:00 completed with no budget_hit (contract-flow 5,892.2 s against 9,000). <!--id:FIX-1185-->
- 🟡 L — **A per-unit statement_timeout needs each unit to be its own top-level statement — evaluate against the unit watchdog before building** — DESIGN-FIRST, deliberately not built by cc-125 (FIX-1128 Half 2 decision H3). statement_timeout is armed once by the server at the start of a TOP-LEVEL statement, so the only way to bound ONE unit of a multi-unit pg_cron procedure is to make that unit its own top-level statement — which means its own backend, via dblink or pg_background, one per unit. That is precisely the resource the 2026-08-29 seizure could not provide: [[FIX-1125]] is the record of the runtime guards being starved of the fork they needed at the moment they were needed. A per-unit bound that costs a backend per unit buys a tighter guarantee by spending the exact resource that runs out first. Evaluate against what already exists before building any of it: enforce_derived_mvs_unit_budget() (FIX-1030) already bounds a unit from OUTSIDE on a */2 cadence and needs no new backend per unit, and refresh_derived_mvs already publishes current_unit, current_unit_index, current_unit_started_at and backend_pid with a COMMIT specifically so that watchdog can see them. The open question is not 'can we bound a unit' but 'is a per-unit in-backend timer worth one backend per unit given that the outside watchdog covers the same case whenever it can fork, and neither covers the case where it cannot'. Note the watchdog's measured lateness in [[FIX-1185]] (4.8 s to 973.9 s past budget on a */2 cadence, six of eleven cancels more than one full cadence late) — that is the real gap a per-unit mechanism would close, and it is the number this evaluation should be run against. Cross-ref [[FIX-1128]] [[FIX-1125]] [[FIX-1030]] [[FIX-1063]]. cc-127 MEASURED transaction_timeout on the clone (PG 17.6, role default, real pg_cron firings) and this bullet's premise does NOT survive: it RE-ARMS PER UNIT — five 2 s units all completed under a 3 s bound, whole CALL 10.039 s, pg_cron succeeded — so an in-backend per-unit bound needs no extra backend after all. The catch is what a unit that EXCEEDS it gets: the backend is TERMINATED, not cancelled. With 4 s units under the same 3 s bound the start row was written and nothing else — no unit row, no terminal row, the EXCEPTION WHEN query_canceled handler never ran — and cron.job_run_details recorded failed with 'server closed the connection unexpectedly' rather than a timeout message, so FIX-1109's succeeded-plus-partial distinction is lost and a reaper would have to write the terminal row the handler no longer can. Both halves verbatim in docs/audits/2026-09-14-fix1185-ceiling.md section 5. Nothing was built on it. <!--id:FIX-1186-->
- 🟡 M — **No durable source for a sitting member's FEC candidate ids — congress-legislators id.fec[] would close the shared-CAND_ID class at source** — FIX-1187 cleans up 151 shared-CAND_ID pairs and carries prior-office ids for five Senators by hand-verified manifest, but nothing stops the class re-forming: the cn{yy} stage mints a candidate stub whenever loadOfficialsByFecIds cannot resolve a CAND_ID at the moment it is read, and an elected row only acquires its id later via the FIX-952 bioguide path or perCycleNameFallback. The unitedstates/congress-legislators dataset publishes id.fec[] — ALL FEC candidate ids per bioguide, including prior-office ones — which is exactly the missing input. With it the cn stage could never mint a stub for a sitting member, and a House-to-Senate move would arrive with both ids already attached instead of needing a manifest. Design-first: it is a new source pipeline, it overlaps the existing congress_officials ingest, and the decision of which side wins on conflict needs stating before any code. Filed by cc-128 from the FIX-1187 design section 7. <!--id:FIX-1189-->
- 🟡 S — **Receipts cron-jobs table lets a job with no data_sync_log writer INHERIT its neighbour's verdict — vacuum jobs reported 'skipped' on someone else's skip_reason** — receipts-daily.ts builds its cron-jobs table with a LATERAL that matches ANY data_sync_log row whose pipeline is LIKE 'pg_cron%' within plus-or-minus 90 seconds of the firing. A job that writes no data_sync_log row of its own therefore picks up whichever neighbouring job's row happens to land in that window, and reports that row's status and skip_reason as its own. Seen in docs/receipts/2026-09-15.md: ec-vacuum-analyze (132.9 s, squarely inside its own 0-140 s band) and officials-vacuum-analyze (3.8 s) both reported 'skipped' carrying financial_entity_totals_refresh's skip_reason. The verdict is not merely cosmetic — it feeds the receipt's health columns, so a genuinely healthy vacuum can read as skipped and a genuinely skipped one can read as fine. A job with no data_sync_log writer must resolve to its cron.job_run_details status alone, and the LATERAL should key on the job, not on a time window. Filed by cc-128 from the FIX-1187 design section 7. CLOSED by cc-133 (0b37ebca, local). Q_CRON_JOBS now keys the data_sync_log LATERAL on the job's DERIVED pipeline - cron.job.command -> the procedure it CALLs -> the pipeline literal in its prosrc, shared with claimProdSession()'s hold set as packages/data/src/lib/cron-job-pipelines.ts - with the +/-90 s window kept only as the disambiguator for the four shared pipelines. A NULL pipeline correlates to nothing by construction, so verdictFor() falls through to the cron status and needed no change; a test feeds it the old inherited shape to record that it was never the bug. WIDER THAN FILED: measured old-vs-new in one prod read session, 12 of 38 jobs had an inherited correlation, not 2, and in every one the borrowed row came from a different pipeline (financial_entity_totals_refresh ten times - fe-crawl runs */30 so its rows blanket the timeline - and entity_connections_rebuild twice). Four of the twelve DO have a pipeline and were borrowing FE's because their own writer had not logged inside the window. Only three verdicts move, all three pipeline-less vacuum jobs that were inheriting; zero non-inherited verdicts change. The table gains a pipeline column. <!--id:FIX-1190-->
- 🔴 M — **A deferred tail relocates its cost into a window the FIX-950 interlock cannot guard — set 1's deferred EC vacuum collided with set 2 and put the front door at 51.7 percent errors** — Measured on the FIX-1187 prod apply (cc-129, 2026-09-17). Two distinct failures of the back-to-back-sets assumption, both caused by what set 1 DEFERRED. First: set 1 deferred 87,206 dead entity_connections tuples to ec-vacuum-analyze (daily 04:30). Set 2 started 04:05:54 and was still in its FR move when that vacuum fired. VACUUM jobs carry NO prod_session_state guard — only the 20 procedures whose prosrc references it are deferred — so the interlock could not coordinate them. Two IO-bound writers on a 2-vCPU box with 256MB shared_buffers: set 2's UPDATE reached 66 minutes for ~26k rows where set 1 had moved 40,649 rows in 265.8s, ec-vacuum-analyze took 3,147.9s against a normal few seconds, fe-vacuum-analyze FAILED at 04:50 with job startup timeout (pg_cron could not fork — a mode recorded at ~0 percent in the 18:00-05:00 UTC band), and edge_logs showed 14x500 + 1x401 of 29 requests = 51.7 percent errors with cancellations at 2.800/min against a 0.033/min baseline. Set 2 was cancelled by operator decision and rolled back atomically; errors fell to 5.1 percent within ten minutes. Second: set 1 deferred 14 entity_connections_rebuild and 7 financial_entity_totals_refresh firings, which resumed the instant the session released at 03:53 — so set 2's first attempt at 04:01 was REFUSED by the preflight with 2 live writers. That refusal is correct and --force was not used, but it means a second set can never follow a first immediately. The FIX-1183 cost table should price a deferred step's collision risk against the NEXT set, and a multi-set landing needs a drain-and-wait step between sets. Evidence in docs/audits/2026-09-17-fix1187-prod-apply.md section 3. cc-130 is the first run to actually take the drain-and-wait readings this bullet asks for, and two of them are unsatisfiable AS WRITTEN. (a) 'no partial EC units, max wall <= 131 s' describes a state prod does not visit: partial is the DESIGNED terminal status of the bounded-crawl arm (unit cap reached, resumable), and over 30 hours prod logged exactly one complete EC unit against dozens of partial and skipped. (c) 'EC n_dead_tup <= 5,000' cannot coexist with 'start >= 90 min clear of 04:25-05:00': EC's only vacuum owner is 04:30 daily, it accumulates ~25,000 dead tuples an hour (0 dead at 05:19:41 to 457,118 at 23:23), so <= 5,000 means starting within ~12 minutes of the vacuum the same rule forbids being near — and the 05:45-09:00 blackout pushes the earliest legal start to 09:00. A usable version needs a ratio, or a vacuum that runs near the window. Readings that DID hold: watchdogs 30/30, 57014 at exactly the 0.033/min baseline, front-door 5xx 0.069 percent. cc-131 (2026-09-18) is this bullet's FIRST SATISFIABLE INSTANCE: eleven of thirteen gates PASS, and both failures are the clock rather than the data (a daily vacuum 31 minutes out at reading time, and the window being Saturday). The two gates cc-130 proved unsatisfiable are replaced and both now read cleanly. 'No partial unit' becomes 'nothing running at start and no unit wall over its own interval' - partial is the crawl arm's DESIGNED terminal status, so the old form failed on correct behaviour; it reads ec-crawl 4/4 at 0.1-0.3 s against a 15-minute interval. 'At most 5,000 dead tuples on EC' becomes a headroom-and-rate gate, (trigger - n_dead_tup) / rate > 2x expected wall, which asks the question that actually matters - can autovacuum trip DURING the set - and reads 65,371 dead tuples of headroom where the old form read 457,118 against 5,000. Readings, all 2026-09-18: unguarded-owner census derived from cron.job joined to pg_proc gives 16 unguarded of 34 active (12 vacuums, both */2 watchdogs, abuse-events-retention, platform-counts-daily); watchdogs 60/60 succeeded in 60 min with zero non-succeeded firings of any job and no startup timeout; prod_session_state clear with 0 live writers; 57014 at 1 per 60 min = 0.0167/min against a 0.033/min baseline; front door 0 of 501 requests 5xx over 15 min. The rate gate carries a caveat it has earned: 0 dead tuples/hour measured across 70 minutes at 03:00-04:00 UTC, the quietest hour, is the weakest possible input to a rate gate even though the collector is provably live, and cc-132 must re-read it INSIDE the Saturday window rather than reuse it. CLOSED by cc-134 (4306bc45, local). Two halves. (i) docs/cc/PROMPT_TEMPLATE.md section Database gains a Drain-and-wait block: cc-131's read 7 (a)-(g) as rules - unguarded-owner clearance derived from cron.job JOIN pg_proc, autovacuum headroom as a ratio from two readings 30 min apart, crawl units against their own interval with partial ALLOWED as a designed terminal status, both */2 watchdogs 60/60, the interlock clear, 57014 at or below 2x the 0.033/min baseline with front-door 5xx at or below 1 percent, and the clock - plus the cc-130 sentence about writing gates from the instrument's own state vocabulary, and the rule that every gate is reported as a number WITH its time. - 🔴 M — **A large FR/EC landing starves pg_cron of workers while its derived EC work drains — both 2-minute watchdogs fail with job startup timeout, so budget enforcement is offline exactly when it is needed** — Measured after the FIX-1187 set-1 prod apply (cc-129, 2026-09-17). Set 1 deleted 87,206 entity_connections money edges and moved 40,649 financial_relationships rows. The incremental EC arm then has to re-derive all of that, one bounded unit per ec-crawl firing. Units that normally run 0-131s grew to 956s and beyond: the 05:30 run was still executing at 16 minutes, longer than the job's own 15-minute interval, so pg_cron queues behind its own overrun and the arm runs effectively continuously. The box saturation that produces is enough that pg_cron CANNOT FORK a worker: cron-job-budget-watchdog and derived-mvs-unit-watchdog both failed with job startup timeout at 05:36, 05:38, 05:42 and 05:44, each after 10-32 seconds, and fe-vacuum-analyze failed the same way at 04:50. Front door measured 19.5 percent errors in the 05:29-05:44 window (19x500 + 12x504 of 164 requests) — worse than the 5.1 percent ten minutes earlier, and this is at the lowest-traffic hour of the day. Two things follow. First, the budget watchdog is the only in-backend bound for a COMMITting pg_cron procedure (see [[FIX-1185]] / [[FIX-1128]]), so a landing that starves the forker disables the one mechanism that would cancel a runaway — the failure mode CLAUDE.md already names as the case the watchdog cannot cover. Second, this is the FIX-1074 convention unpaid: a landing owns the DERIVED work its rewrite creates, not just the watermark correctness of it, and neither the FIX-1183 cost table nor --defer-tails prices the post-landing EC drain at all. Candidate handling: drain the EC arm under the landing's own session before releasing it, or widen p_max_units for a bounded catch-up window, or hold the interlock until the derived backlog is measured clear. Evidence in docs/audits/2026-09-17-fix1187-prod-apply.md sections 3 and 4, and cross-refs [[FIX-1191]] [[FIX-1193]]. cc-130 WIDENS this: the starvation is not specific to a landing's derived EC work. Of 158 job startup timeout failures on 2026-09-17, 117 fell in 04:00-09:00 (set 1's drain, the known mode) but 41 fell in 14:00-15:00 — during contract-flow-rollups-refresh, an ORDINARY SCHEDULED JOB with no landing anywhere near it. In the 15:00 hour alone cron-job-budget-watchdog failed to fork 14 times. So any sufficiently long job starves the forker, and the bullet's ownership question is broader than a landing's post-hoc EC drain. For scale on the drain itself: set 1's donations_incr_windows arm crawled windows 2/16 to 13/16 from 05:30 to 11:30 at 410-956 s per unit and 27k-32k rows each, then the entity_connection_stats_windows arm restarted at window 1/16 at 14:45 (1,887.1 s, 0 rows) and reached 15/16 by 23:15. cc-131 (2026-09-18) adds the follow-through on the fe-vacuum-analyze casualty this bullet already names. It failed 2026-09-17 04:50 with job startup timeout and is the ONLY non-succeeded firing of any vacuum job in nine days; FE's last_vacuum is consequently still 2026-09-16 04:50 with vacuum_count 1. FE happens to be fine at 99.9 percent all-visible so the miss cost little this time, but the shape is the point - the landing's starvation removes the maintenance job that exists to clean up after the landing, which is the FIX-1074 ownership question in its sharpest form. Alongside it, ec-vacuum-analyze ran 3,147.9 s (52m 28s) that same morning against 9-133 s on each of the eight preceding days, a 24-350x blow-up with set 1's churn the only candidate, and the 09-17 receipts show that job SKIPPED under the FIX-950 interlock the day set 1 landed. For scale on the other side: by 2026-09-18 02:49 the drain has fully caught up - ec-crawl units run 0.1-0.3 s and fe-crawl 0.1-0.2 s, with zero dead-tuple accumulation measured over 70 minutes, so the post-landing backlog clears in well under two days. NO CHANGE from cc-133's hold work, checked rather than assumed: the two every-two-minutes watchdogs (enforce_cron_job_budgets, enforce_derived_mvs_unit_budget) are UNGUARDED - they do not consult prod_session_state() and they INSERT no data_sync_log row of their own, so they resolve no pipeline in [[FIX-1190]]'s mapping and are not in the [[FIX-1172]] hold set. A supervised session therefore neither defers them nor suppresses anything about them, which is the right answer: the watchdog is the one instrument that must keep running while a human holds the box. APPEND 2026-09-19 (cc-132, THE COUNTERFACTUAL THIS BULLET NEEDED: a comparable landing that starved nothing): the census asked for here is now read. Across 2026-09-17 00:00 UTC to 2026-09-19 19:40 UTC there are 158 job startup timeout failures and EVERY ONE falls on 2026-09-17 — zero on 09-18, zero on 09-19. By hour on 09-17: 04:00 26 (watchdogs) + 4 others, 05:00 31 + 3, 06:00 10, 07:00 18, 08:00 17 + 2, 09:00 6, 14:00 8, 15:00 29 + 4; watchdog subtotal 145 of 158, the rest ec-crawl 7, fe-crawl 4, abuse-events-retention 1, fe-vacuum-analyze 1. So the answer to 'how many outside any landing' is, for these three days, none: the 04:00-09:00 cluster is set 1's drain and the 14:00-15:00 cluster is contract-flow-rollups-refresh, which is cc-130's widening and still stands — but both are LONG JOBS, and the two quiet days either side produced not a single fork failure. THE COUNTERFACTUAL. FIX-1187 set 2 landed on prod 2026-09-19 18:50:32 to 19:17:25 UTC and starved nothing: both */2 watchdogs forked 22/22 from 18:50 onward with ZERO startup timeouts and max wall 0.34 s / 0.15 s, ec-crawl 3/3 and fe-crawl 2/2 all succeeded at max 0.39 s, the 60-minute 57014 census read 1 cancellation total (0.0167/min = 0.51x the 0.033/min baseline) and the front door 0 of 124 requests 5xx. Set 2 moved 46,790 FR rows against set 1's 99,217 — 47 percent of the volume — but the three differences that matter are structural, not scalar: FIX-1192 cut the _collision build from 5,268.7 s to 110.1 s, --defer-tails deferred 14 of 17 tail steps so no platform-scoped rebuild ran (the FIX-1165 rule), and the manifest-scoped tails ran chunked at 244.3 s / ~330 s / ~520 s rather than as single statements. That is evidence the starvation is driven by LONG SINGLE STATEMENTS holding a backend, not by rewrite volume as such, which sharpens Monday's design question: the lever is statement duration and tail scope, not p_max_units. One caveat against over-reading it: set 2's derived EC work had not begun at the time of this measurement — ec_crawl_gate() was in cycle_cooldown with 256 of 360 minutes elapsed — so this counterfactual covers the LANDING and its manifest-scoped tails, not the EC drain that follows. Sat 2026-09-19 through Mon 2026-09-21 ~23:45 UTC: ZERO 'job startup timeout' failures on any job, and ZERO cron_job_budget_action rows. The set-2 landing on 09-19 and Sunday's pushes produced no bursts at all. Note for the P1 input: cc-141 REFUTED the jobid-17 restart hypothesis (see [[FIX-969]]), so the two Tuesday restarts are not attributable to a pg_cron job and are not evidence of a job taking the box down. APPEND 2026-09-22 (cc-142, P2-A IS LIVE): both */2 watchdogs now have a SECOND firing path that needs no pg_cron worker. `/api/cron/cron-watchdog` runs on a Vercel cron at `*/2` and calls `public.run_cron_watchdogs()` through PostgREST; pg_cron's two jobs are UNCHANGED and still fire, so this is belt AND braces and both paths are idempotent by construction (the ledger's `runid` PK, and the unit watchdog's `metadata ? 'watchdog_canceled_at'`). THE DESIGN'S PREMISE WAS WRONG AND THE TREE SAID SO: the route could not call `enforce_cron_job_budgets()` directly as §3 P2-A assumed. Measured on prod 2026-09-21 — both functions are SECURITY INVOKER (`prosecdef=f`) and owned by `postgres`; `service_role` DOES hold EXECUTE on both (Supabase's default grant, which survived because every REVOKE in the family names PUBLIC/anon/authenticated and never service_role) but has `has_schema_privilege('service_role','cron','USAGE')=false` and `pg_has_role('service_role','pg_signal_backend','MEMBER')=false`, so as INVOKER the read dies at the cron schema gate (the table SELECT grant is present and unreachable without schema USAGE) and the cancel would be refused anyway. `postgres` is NOT superuser (`rolsuper=f`) but IS a member of `pg_signal_backend`, which is the actual mechanism FIX-1030's comment describes. Hence one narrow SECURITY DEFINER wrapper: no parameters, no pid input, it CALLS both functions rather than copying either, and it cancels only what their own unchanged predicates already select — so it is not the "cancel any backend" primitive FIX-1030 refused. `cron_job_budget_action.acted_via` ('pg_cron' | 'vercel', NOT NULL DEFAULT 'pg_cron') records which path acted; a 'vercel' row in a window where pg_cron's firings show `job startup timeout` is THE RECEIPT this item is waiting for, and FIX-1073's signal is the trigger to go look. Proven end-to-end on the clone's live scheduler before the push: a scratch job over a 5 s budget was cancelled through local PostgREST (HTTP 200/122 ms, canceled=1, pid 3292, run 17435 -> failed, ledger row acted_via='vercel'), the same call with nothing over budget returned canceled=0 and wrote nothing, and the same call as anon was refused 42501. Receipts §9 "The forker" renders the startup-timeout hour/day buckets, the longest job overlapping each burst hour (threshold 3), and the cancels with their path. Also folded in from FIX-1063 (closed, archived — no live bullet to note on): its watchdog now has this second firing path, and its ledger has gained a column. NOT BUILT, deliberately: nothing alerts on `cron_job_budget_action` — the ledger is written and now rendered, but no canary condition reads it. That is the candidate this append names and does not build. APPEND 2026-09-22 (cc-142, P2-A IS DEPLOYED BUT NOT YET FIRING — see [[FIX-1207]]): the half of P2-A that can be proven from a shell is proven; the half that matters is not. The route answers 401 unauthenticated where a nonexistent sibling 404s, so it is live and gated; the wrapper returns HTTP 200 in 461 ms from prod PostgREST with checked=0, which is the proof the DEFINER's cron read works. But pg_stat_statements on the pgrst_source wrapper call reads 1 at 01:19:58 (my one manual call), 2 at 01:22:20, and still 2 at 01:28:16, 01:29:42 and 01:37:35 — ONE route invocation ever, roughly seven */2 slots missed — while front-door-watch fired on time at 01:00:28, 01:15:28 and 01:30:28 in the same window. So this is specific to the new cron, not to Vercel or the plan. Until FIX-1207 resolves it, treat P2-A as INERT: the second firing path exists in code and does not run, which is the same shape as the failure it was built for. Do not read this bullet's own receipt condition (a 'vercel' row during a startup-timeout burst) as pending-but-healthy — it cannot occur at all while the schedule is dead. CORRECTION 2026-09-22 (cc-142, supersedes the 'NOT YET FIRING' append immediately above it): THE CRON IS FIRING. Vercel's function log shows */2 exactly — 19:08:18, 19:10:18, 19:12:18, 19:14:18 PDT (02:08–02:14 UTC), each GET 200 logging 'budget checked=0 canceled=0 unit=none via=vercel'. [[FIX-1207]], which claimed it was dead, is closed as a no-op; it was my pg_stat_statements proxy that was blind, not the schedule. So P2-A is DEPLOYED AND RUNNING: the wrapper is on Pro, the route fires every two minutes, and each firing gets a well-formed payload out of run_cron_watchdogs() (via=vercel is the function's own literal). The deployment targets the right database — NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_URL greps out of the production client bundle as xsazcoxinpgttgquwvuf. One residual doubt is tracked in [[FIX-1208]] and is worth knowing before anyone declares victory: those route calls leave NO trace in this database's pg_stat_statements although an identical direct curl does, which is either a harmless instrument quirk or, less likely but more serious, evidence the call is not landing here at all. THE RECEIPT THIS BULLET ALREADY WAITS FOR SETTLES BOTH QUESTIONS AT ONCE, at zero cost: the first time anything on prod goes over budget, cron_job_budget_action.acted_via says 'vercel' or 'pg_cron', which simultaneously proves the second firing path reaches this database and shows which guard won the race. APPEND 2026-09-22 (cc-143, note only -- P2-A's second path is getting a liveness receipt, no change to P2-A itself): [[FIX-1208]]'s stamp is on trunk as 20260920090000, so once it reaches Pro the question "did the Vercel path fire?" becomes answerable on a QUIET day from pipeline_state.cron_watchdog_vercel, which acted_via structurally cannot answer -- acted_via is written only on a cancel, so P2-A's own receipt existed for the incident and not for the ordinary day. - 🟡 S — **canary-check condition keys have no census — a new key is enforced nowhere** — cc-133 section 7(a) went looking for the place a new canary condition key gets registered and found there is none. detector-coverage.test.ts is DB-backed and self-skipping and is about the FIX-977 registry-superset-census property; it carries no condition-key list and cannot go red for an unregistered key. The only registration anywhere is a three-line stability assertion in canary-prod-session.test.ts (assert.equal(KEY_OVERRUN, 'prod_session_overrun') and its two siblings), which pins the VALUE of three keys somebody already thought to write down and says nothing about the other twenty. So FIX-1190's new prod_session_hold_stale key never went red before it was added, because nothing in the tree can make it. The keys live in five shapes across canary-check.ts - string literals, template prefixes with a dynamic suffix, imported KEY_* constants from canary-prod-session.ts and canary-fec-drop.ts, and one local variable resolved from a three-way state ternary - so a census has to read the source rather than import a list. Cross-refs FIX-977 (the registry-superset property this is the missing half of), FIX-1177 and FIX-1172 (whose key is the one that surfaced it). <!--id:FIX-1197-->
- 🟢 S — **audit-fec-emit-residue --out-dir default is cwd-relative — writes under packages/data when run through pnpm --filter** — The default is path.join('docs','audits'), which resolves against the CWD, and pnpm --filter @civitics/data runs in packages/data — so every unqualified run writes packages/data/docs/audits/ instead of the repo-root docs/audits/ that every reader looks in, and the run still reports success. Three stray FIX-1106 residue TSVs are sitting there now, untracked and not gitignored. receipts-daily.ts already solved this by walking up for pnpm-workspace.yaml (FIX-1176); reuse that. Cross-refs FIX-1106, FIX-1183.  APPEND 2026-09-19 (cc-135, CLOSED, local): fixed by lifting receipts-daily.ts's walk-up into packages/data/src/lib/repo-root.ts and pointing both callers at it, so there is one copy rather than two that drift. The test asserts the property that was actually violated rather than the string -- the resolved default is absolute, equals <repoRoot>/docs/audits, is not under packages/data, and is UNCHANGED when the process chdirs into packages/data, which is exactly where pnpm --filter puts it. The three stray 2026-09-14 FIX-1106 residue TSVs under packages/data/docs/audits/ were left in place, untracked: they are real audit output and deleting them is a separate call. <!--id:FIX-1198-->
- 🟠 S — **refresh-disposable-emails monthly workflow has failed at push since 2026-09-03 — the repo pre-push hook aborts create-pull-request** — Run 33769138373 (2026-09-03, schedule) failed with '[pre-push] fixes:check:trunk failed — push aborted' then 'failed to push some refs'. The root prepare script wires core.hooksPath=.githooks on pnpm install, so peter-evans/create-pull-request's git push runs the repo's own pre-push hook inside CI, where fixes:check:trunk cannot pass (shallow checkout, no trunk ref). The 08-03 run predates that wiring and succeeded. Consequence: the branch chore/refresh-disposable-emails was frozen at its 08-03 content for six weeks and the blocklist went unrefreshed; cc-135 landed that content by hand as e738116a and closed PR #3, but 2026-10-03 fails the same way unless the workflow either skips hooks for its own push or does not run the install that wires hooksPath. Note the hook is doing its job — the fix is to scope it, not to disable it. Cross-refs FIX-462, FIX-794.  APPEND 2026-09-19 (cc-136, CLOSED, local): shipped as ad623386. THE BULLET'S DIAGNOSIS WAS WRONG IN ITS MECHANISM, and the real one is worse. Run 33769138373 did not fail because no trunk ref resolved. actions/checkout@v4 fetched +808fc5a2:refs/remotes/origin/main at --depth=1, so origin/main resolves fine -- it just reaches exactly one commit. The failure text names FIX-960 (54d9206d, aa278b7a) and FIX-962 (09ab3ab9) as recorded-as-done-but-never-shipped; all three are ancestors of origin/main today and were on 2026-09-03. A false positive. The mechanism: create-pull-request fetches its long-lived branch at --depth=11, that branch was cut from main on 2026-08-03, so its ancestry IS early-August main and those objects become present while origin/main stays depth 1 -- and merge-base --is-ancestor then answers false for genuine ancestors. Reproduced on a real depth-1 clone 2026-09-19: fetch chore/refresh-disposable-emails at depth 11, 09ab3ab9 present, origin/main still 1 commit, is-ancestor exits 1. Filed as [[FIX-1200]]. The receipts bot passes the same hook only by luck -- its object store is depth 1 so every done.log SHA classifies unknown and the gate is inert (nightly run 35403626528, trailersScanned 0). So in CI the gate was never meaningful in either direction. The fix scopes the TRUNK gate only: under GITHUB_ACTIONS, when the repo is shallow or no trunk ref resolves, it prints a reason and steps aside. The typecheck gate is untouched and still runs. Not --no-verify (drops typecheck too, and for humans), not --ignore-scripts (unwires the hook for every other consumer of pnpm install). scripts/test-pre-push-hook.mjs, 15 assertions, pins the scope in both directions. RECEIPT: dispatch run 35415836771 (2026-09-19 02:30 UTC, workflow_dispatch on main) SUCCEEDED in 49 s; its log carries 'fixes:check:trunk SKIPPED under GITHUB_ACTIONS: shallow checkout' immediately after 'typecheck clean', the push to chore/refresh-disposable-emails went through, and PR #4 opened (+698/-1 across the two allowed paths) -- six weeks of upstream drift, awaiting Craig's review per the workflow's own design. The SCHEDULED receipt is still the 2026-10-03 11:00 UTC run; a dispatch tests the push path, not the schedule. APPEND 2026-09-20 (cc-138): the guard now self-detects, and this bullet's early exit is belt-and-braces rather than the mechanism. [[FIX-1200]] moved the shallow-clone test inside classifySha(), where it is computed once per run and yields unknown instead of not-ancestor — so the CI false-positive class is fixed in the tool and the gate would be correct under GITHUB_ACTIONS even without the hook stepping aside. The early exit is KEPT: it does not depend on the guard being right, and it saves the typecheck minute on every CI push that could not have learned anything from the check. What changed is its meaning, and the hook's comment now says so. <!--id:FIX-1199-->
- 🟡 S — **fixes-sync trunk guard cannot tell 'not an ancestor' from 'trunk is shallow so I cannot tell' — classifySha keys its escape hatch on object presence, not on trunk reachability** — Surfaced by [[FIX-1199]]. scripts/fixes-sync.mjs classifySha() returns 'unknown' (and is skipped) only when git cat-file -e fails, i.e. when the OBJECT is absent. It never asks whether TRUNK is reachable. In a shallow checkout git rev-list origin/main yields one commit and git merge-base --is-ancestor answers 1 for genuine ancestors, so any done.log SHA that happens to be present is reported as an off-trunk violation. Reproduced 2026-09-19 on a real depth-1 clone: after a depth-11 fetch of a sibling branch (what peter-evans/create-pull-request does), 09ab3ab9 is present, origin/main still resolves 1 commit, and merge-base --is-ancestor 09ab3ab9 origin/main exits 1 although the full clone answers ancestor. That is exactly run 33769138373, which named FIX-960 and FIX-962 as recorded-but-never-shipped. FIX-1199 scoped the pre-push hook so CI no longer reaches this path, and fixes-integrity.yml checks out fetch-depth 0, so there is no live consumer today — but the guard is still wrong for any shallow clone and the failure mode is a confident false accusation rather than an error. Fix: have classifySha (or resolveTrunkRef) consult git rev-parse --is-shallow-repository and downgrade every not-ancestor verdict to unknown when trunk is shallow, with a warn line naming the degradation, so the guard fails open loudly instead of closed wrongly. Test alongside scripts/test-trunk-guard.mjs. Cross-refs [[FIX-461]], [[FIX-1199]]. CLOSED 2026-09-20 (cc-138). classifySha() now computes trunk reachability ONCE per run and classifies every SHA unknown — never not-ancestor — when the repository is shallow or no trunk ref resolves, printing one reason line. The old escape was per-SHA and keyed on git cat-file -e, i.e. on whether the OBJECT was present, which is precisely what the failing shape defeats: a depth-1 clone that then fetches a SIBLING branch more deeply has the objects AND a resolving origin/main while rev-list origin/main yields ONE commit, so merge-base --is-ancestor answered 1 for genuine ancestors (run 33769138373 accused FIX-960 and FIX-962, all of whose SHAs were on main). PREDICATE CHOSEN: git rev-parse --is-shallow-repository, NOT rev-list --count >= the oldest done.log SHA's distance — the count test needs the history it is trying to prove it has, and in the failing case that count is 1, so any distance derived from it is meaningless rather than merely small; the truncated-before-that-SHA case is already covered by the per-SHA cat-file escape, which stays for the non-shallow path. Drift detection is untouched: unknown already means no evidence either way downstream, evaluateTrunkViolations skips an ID whose SHAs are all unknown, and the FIX-461 filter only drops on an explicit not-ancestor. The test builds cc-136's exact fixture and asserts BOTH halves of it — the clone really is shallow and origin/main really is one commit, AND an on-trunk SHA's object really is present — because without those the case would pass against the old code; paired with the inverse, a full clone with a real off-trunk SHA, which must and does still accuse. Two harness bugs surfaced building it, both of which had made the fixture lie: git clone --depth is SILENTLY IGNORED for a local-path source (it needs file://, with three slashes on Windows), and a --depth deeper than the whole history un-shallows the repo outright; and runScript threw stderr away on a SUCCESSFUL run, so a warning printed by a script that exits 0 was invisible to every assertion. The pre-push hook keeps its CI early exit — it is an optimisation now rather than a workaround, and the comment says so. Cross-ref [[FIX-1199]], [[FIX-461]]. <!--id:FIX-1200-->
- 🟠 S — **TAIL_OWNERS still hardcodes fr-vacuum-analyze as Mon 01:00 — FIX-1193 fixed the tail cost table but not the operator-facing deferred-tail banner** — FIX-1193 replaced the tail cost table's hardcoded owner parentheticals with columns read live from cron.job, and it is correct. But it did not reach TAIL_OWNERS in packages/data/src/scripts/fec-orphan-classify.ts around line 1021, a separate exported constant whose vacuum entry is the literal string 'fr-vacuum-analyze  financial_relationships  Mon 01:00'. printDeferredTail() prints it, and four remediation scripts call that: merge-same-person-official-dupes, remediate-cross-person-misattribution, remediate-role-ineligible-holders and remediate-fec-emit-residue. FIX-1191 moved fr-vacuum-analyze to 0 3 * * * on prod on 2026-09-19, so that string is now WRONG, and it was printed wrong by the FIX-1187 set-2 apply itself at 18:5x UTC the same evening, about 40 minutes before the migration landed. Two more copies of the same stale fact sit in remediate-fec-emit-residue.ts, in the module header around line 30 and in the over-threshold console warning around line 211, both reading 'fr-vacuum-analyze (Mon 01:00)'. The operator-facing banner is exactly where a stale owner schedule does damage: it is the line an operator reads to decide whether deferring the vacuum is safe, and it now understates the collection by six days in the wrong direction. Fix is the FIX-1193 one applied a second time, since readOwnerSchedules() already exists and the tail table already proves the pattern: have printDeferredTail() take the live Map and render schedule and guarded per owner rather than carrying prose. Cross-ref [[FIX-1193]] [[FIX-1191]] [[FIX-1165]]. CLOSED 2026-09-20 (cc-138). TAIL_OWNERS no longer carries schedule text at all: each entry is a job NAME plus what that job collects, and printDeferredTail(kind, owners?) renders the schedule and guarded columns through ownerColumns() — the SAME function FIX-1193's tail cost table uses, imported rather than re-derived, so the banner and the table cannot disagree about what ? / NOT SCHEDULED / [INACTIVE] mean. The owners map comes from readOwnerSchedulesOnce(client), a per-client memoised readOwnerSchedules: the banner is printed from runVacuum / runMvsAndVacuum / runRollups, which are reached on several paths and NOT always after main() has read the owners, so threading the map through ~20 call sites would have passed it through unchanged at most of them; memoising keeps the one-query-per-run property and leaves printDeferredTail a pure function of its arguments, which is what makes it testable with stubbed rows. cc-132 section 9g said two more copies sit in remediate-fec-emit-residue.ts and THAT IS NOT WHAT THE TREE HELD: that script already calls printTailTable(..., owners) and its only stale copy was one doc comment; the real second copy was a STALE UNTIL THEIR OWN SCHEDULE string literal carrying Sun + Wed 08:00 UTC, present in BOTH merge-same-person-official-dupes.ts and remediate-cross-person-misattribution.ts, and the source-scan test found the second one on its first run. Those two name the workflow (rebuild-entity-connections.yml) instead of a clock, because their owner is a GitHub Actions schedule and there is no cron.job row to read live. The guard is a source scan asserting no (Mon|Tue|...) HH:MM or daily HH:MM literal survives in packages/data/src/scripts/*.ts outside comments — it fails against the pre-change tree, which is the point — plus render cases for live, INACTIVE, NOT SCHEDULED and no-map-at-all. Cross-ref [[FIX-1193]], [[FIX-1191]]. <!--id:FIX-1201-->
- 🔴 S — **FIX-1194 P2-A second firing path is deployed but its Vercel cron is not observably firing at */2** — Measured 2026-09-22 01:19-01:37 UTC, immediately after cc-142 landed P2-A. The route IS deployed: GET /api/cron/cron-watchdog unauthenticated returns 401 {error Unauthorized} while a nonexistent sibling path returns 404 HTML, so the code is live on the production alias and the CRON_SECRET gate is wired. The wrapper IS working: one manual call through prod PostgREST with the service key returned HTTP 200 in 461 ms with checked=0 canceled=0, and checked=0 rather than an error is the proof the SECURITY DEFINER read of cron.job_run_details succeeds through PostgREST. What is NOT happening is the schedule. pg_stat_statements counts the pgrst_source wrapper call: 1 at 01:19:58 (my manual call at 01:17:12), 2 at 01:22:20, and still 2 at 01:28:16, 01:29:42 and 01:37:35 - so exactly ONE route invocation ever occurred, around 01:20-01:22, and roughly seven subsequent */2 slots produced nothing. This is not a Vercel-wide outage and not a plan limitation: front-door-watch fired normally at 01:00:28, 01:15:28 and 01:30:28 in the same window and writes a data_sync_log row each time, and */15 and */30 entries have worked since FIX-1127/1130 so sub-daily crons are allowed on this plan. Leading hypothesis is registration latency for a NEWLY ADDED vercel.json cron entry, with the single observed invocation being the registration fire; the alternative is a per-plan minimum interval above two minutes that is silently clamping */2. Neither is decidable from the shell because the Vercel dashboard and vercel ls need device OAuth (cc-140). WHY IT MATTERS AT THIS SEVERITY: P2-A exists so that budget enforcement survives a pg_cron fork starvation, and a second firing path that does not fire is worth exactly nothing - the whole item is inert until this is resolved, while looking shipped. NEXT STEP: read the Vercel dashboard cron list for the project and confirm whether /api/cron/cron-watchdog is registered and what its last invocation was; if it is registered but throttled, widen the schedule to the smallest interval the plan honours and say so in docs/OPERATIONS.md; if it is not registered, redeploy and re-measure with the same pgss counter. Re-measure with: SELECT calls FROM pg_stat_statements WHERE query ILIKE '%pgrst_source%' AND query ILIKE '%run_cron_watchdogs%'. Filed by cc-142 from its own item-4 deploy check. Cross-refs [[FIX-1194]] [[FIX-1125]] [[FIX-1137]]. CLOSED AS NO-OP 2026-09-22 (cc-142, same session that filed it — the premise is FALSE). Craig pasted the Vercel function log within the hour: the cron fires at exactly */2 — 19:08:18, 19:10:18, 19:12:18, 19:14:18 PDT, each GET 200 carrying the route's own line 'budget checked=0 canceled=0 unit=none via=vercel'. Those are PDT, so 02:08–02:14 UTC — the same minutes in which I read pg_stat_statements and reported 'still 2, not firing'. The cron was firing THROUGH my measurement window and my instrument never moved. WHAT I GOT WRONG, because the method is the reusable part: I used a pg_stat_statements counter as a proxy for 'did the route run', validated it against ONE direct curl, and never validated it against the thing I was actually measuring — a call from the deployed route. A proxy confirmed on path A and then trusted on path B is not an instrument, it is an assumption; and this one failed in the direction that manufactures a 🔴 out of a healthy system. The control I did read (front-door-watch firing at 01:00/01:15/01:30) correctly told me Vercel cron worked in general, which should have raised my suspicion of the counter rather than my confidence in the finding. The residual question — why route RPC calls leave no pg_stat_statements trace when an identical curl does, and whether that means the call is not reaching prod at all — is real, is NOT settled, and moves to [[FIX-1208]] with the full measurement set. The decisive evidence for that one arrives free: cron_job_budget_action.acted_via names the path on the first prod cancel. <!--id:FIX-1207-->
- 🟡 S — **Vercel route RPC calls are invisible in pg_stat_statements while an identical direct curl is counted — P2-A works but cannot be verified by that instrument** — Surfaced by cc-142 while wrongly concluding the FIX-1194 P2-A cron was dead (see [[FIX-1207]], closed no-op). MEASURED on prod 2026-09-22 02:12-02:22 UTC. (1) The Vercel cron DOES fire at exactly */2 - function log shows 19:08:18 19:10:18 19:12:18 19:14:18 PDT (= 02:08-02:14 UTC), each GET 200 logging the route's own verdict line 'budget checked=0 canceled=0 unit=none via=vercel'. (2) via=vercel and checked=0 are read out of the RPC's returned jsonb, so run_cron_watchdogs() executed and returned a well-formed payload - a null or absent payload renders 'UNPARSEABLE' and an RPC error logs 'RPC failed' instead. (3) The deployment targets the right database: NEXT_PUBLIC_SUPABASE_URL is inlined into the client bundle and greps out of the production chunks as https://xsazcoxinpgttgquwvuf.supabase.co, and createAdminClient() reads that same var. (4) AND YET the pgrst_source pg_stat_statements row for run_cron_watchdogs does not move for route calls. It sat at exactly 2 calls / 38.1 ms across seven readings spanning 01:22-02:17 while the cron fired ~25 times; calling the deployed route by hand with CRON_SECRET returned ok:true elapsed_ms:11 x-vercel-cache:BYPASS and moved it not at all; a direct curl to the same REST endpoint with the same service key moved it 2 -> 3 (38.1 -> 56.2 ms) immediately. A whole-database snapshot diff across a window containing one hand route call found 13 rows moving and NONE of them a PostgREST run_cron_watchdogs row - the movers were pgbouncer.get_auth, DISCARD ALL, the cron.job_run_details bookkeeping, pg_cron's own SELECT public.enforce_cron_job_budgets() and enforce_derived_mvs_unit_budget(), and my own queries. pg_stat_statements is healthy and not the problem in general (stats_reset 2026-09-15, top row 10,700 calls, track=top so the PostgREST statement is top-level and should be counted); track_functions is 'none' so pg_stat_user_functions offers no second counter; I hold pg_read_all_stats and there are zero '<insufficient privilege>' rows, so it is not a visibility filter. TWO READINGS REMAIN POSSIBLE and they differ in consequence: either supabase-js's request headers make PostgREST emit different SQL under a queryid the sweep somehow missed (harmless - P2-A works, the instrument is just wrong), or the route's call is not reaching this database at all despite the env var (serious - P2-A is inert and the watchdog logs all-clear forever without checking). WHY IT IS NOT URGENT: the decisive evidence arrives free. cron_job_budget_action.acted_via records which path cancelled, so the first time anything on prod goes over budget the ledger says 'vercel' or 'pg_cron' and settles it - that is the same receipt [[FIX-1194]] already waits for, now doing double duty. Until then treat P2-A as probably-working-but-unproven and do NOT use pg_stat_statements to verify any Vercel route's RPC. NEXT STEPS if it needs forcing rather than waiting: add a cheap distinguishable observable to the wrapper's return that the route logs (it already returns 'at' - log it, and a timestamp that advances proves a live call), or reproduce the header difference locally against the clone by calling through supabase-js vs curl and diffing pg_stat_statements there, which costs nothing and is where this should have been characterised in the first place. Cross-refs [[FIX-1207]] [[FIX-1194]]. APPEND 2026-09-22 (cc-143, INSTRUMENT BUILT AND ON TRUNK -- PRO PENDING, STAYS OPEN): 20260920090000_fix1208_cron_watchdog_vercel_liveness.sql gives run_cron_watchdogs() a liveness stamp -- pipeline_state.cron_watchdog_vercel = {at, checked, canceled, unit}, rewritten on EVERY call rather than only when something is cancelled. That is the whole difference from cron_job_budget_action.acted_via, which is a CHANGE proxy: it is written only when the wrapper re-labels a row, and it only re-labels on a cancel, so on a healthy day -- 719 of every 720 calls -- it is silent, and its silence cannot be told apart from the route never having run. acted_via can prove the path ACTED; it can never prove the path is ALIVE, which is exactly what this bullet was filed about. The row is a Vercel receipt BY CONSTRUCTION rather than by trust: pg_cron calls enforce_cron_job_budgets() and enforce_derived_mvs_unit_budget() DIRECTLY, by name, in its own two */2 job commands, and this wrapper's only caller is the Vercel route, so there is no code path by which a row stamped inside it could have come from pg_cron. The call graph does the work and nothing about the instrument has to be believed. THE STAMP SITS IN ITS OWN EXCEPTION HANDLER, which is the one decision here worth arguing about: this function's existing discipline is that each inner watchdog runs inside its own handler so one failing never blinds the other, and an instrument must obey that rule more strictly than the things it measures -- an unhandled error in the stamp would take down the CANCEL path P2-A exists to keep alive, and a watchdog that dies because its odometer jammed is worse than no odometer. A failed stamp surfaces as stamp_error in the payload and the watchdogs' results return regardless. Dead-tuple math stated rather than hoped: 720 updates a day on a 37-row table whose FIX-1003b reloptions are threshold-led (trigger = 20 + 0.05 x 37 = 21.85), so about 33 extra autovacuum cycles a day of milliseconds each -- roughly tripling pipeline_state's write volume and vacuum cadence from a very small base (prod read 2026-09-22: 37 rows, 16 dead, 1,997 lifetime updates, 97 autovacuums against the 09-15 epoch, i.e. ~307 updates and ~15 autovacuums a day), in the cheapest shape available: ONE row, rewritten in place, never appended to. The route's verdict line gains at=<iso> so the Vercel log itself carries the DB clock and the two instruments can be compared without a query; a payload without at prints at=? rather than at=undefined or dropping the segment, because a missing clock is exactly the reading that matters when the stamp is also missing. Receipts section 9 gains ONE line from a new forker_vercel_liveness reader key: the stamp's age, and missing -- the existing verdict vocabulary, rule 48, not a new word -- above 10 minutes, ten because the route fires every 2 minutes so five missed firings is the smallest window that is not ordinary Vercel cron jitter or a redeploy; the absent-key branch says WHY it might be absent so a reader never has to guess between "migration not applied" and "route not running". LOCAL PROOF taken through PostgREST rather than psql, because the route's path is what is under test: two calls 11 s apart returned at 03:45:31.963347 and 03:45:42.444542, no stamp_error either time, and pipeline_state held exactly ONE row whose at and updated_at advanced together. PRO PENDING: cron_watchdog_vercel was confirmed ABSENT on prod at 03:48 UTC, which is the pre-push baseline, and the push is blocked behind [[FIX-1178]] rider (c)'s 7-day counter gate, opening 2026-09-22 15:09:48 UTC. This closes on a Pattern B record commit once two prod stamps two minutes apart have actually been READ -- not before, and not on the migration landing. Whether pg_stat_statements stays blind to the route's RPC is then a curiosity rather than a defect, per the filing. Cross-ref [[FIX-1194]]. APPEND 2026-09-23 (cc-145, NOT CLOSED: THE STAMP NEVER APPEARED, AND edge_logs DECIDES BETWEEN THIS BULLET'S TWO READINGS): 20260920090000 reached Pro at 2026-09-23 00:03:00 UTC (run_cron_watchdogs prod against local is 0 lines; before the push the delta was the stamp block only). pipeline_state.cron_watchdog_vercel had NO row at 00:03:19, 00:08:07 or 00:14:03, across the */2 firings from 00:04 to 00:14, so the Pattern B close did not happen. A third instrument, independent of pg_stat_statements: this project's Supabase edge_logs show exactly THREE POST /rest/v1/rpc/run_cron_watchdogs requests in the whole retained window. They are 2026-09-22 01:17:11 (user_agent node, no x-client-info), 01:20:18 (x-client-info supabase-js-node/2.99.1, the route's client signature) and 02:22:19 (no x-client-info, consistent with the direct curl that moved pgss 2 to 3). There are none since, including none from 00:00 to 00:15 UTC on 09-23. Over the same window the sibling Vercel crons reach the same gateway on schedule: front-door-watch POST data_sync_log at 23:45:34 and 00:00:31, platform-snapshot POST platform_usage_snapshot at 00:01:22 and its get_supabase_cpu_max RPC at 00:00:30. So the SERIOUS reading holds: the route's RPC is not reaching this database, and P2-A has been inert since about 01:20 UTC on 09-22 while its function log reports budget checked=0 canceled=0. LEADING CANDIDATE, NOT PROVEN: the Next 14 Data Cache is answering an identical-body POST fetch from the entry the 01:20:18 call created. See the FIX-878 lesson in project memory. The hand route call returned ok:true in 11 ms, too fast for a round trip to us-west-2. front-door-watch fetches with cache no-store; this route's supabase client does not. Against it: platform-snapshot's fixed-argument RPCs do reach the gateway, so the difference is not proven. THE DECIDING READ IS CRAIG'S: since 8cc9822 the route logs the DB clock as at=. If every Vercel log line carries the same at= (about 2026-09-22T01:20 UTC), the payload is cached. The likely fix is one line (a no-store fetch on this route's client, or export const fetchCache = force-no-store); after it, this closes on two stamps. Until then, treat [[FIX-1194]] P2-A as NOT DEPLOYED. APPEND 2026-09-23 (cc-146, CLOSED ON PROD — IT WAS THE DATA CACHE): Craig's Vercel log paste, seven consecutive */2 lines 18:54-19:06 PDT on 09-22, all carried at=2026-09-22T01:20:18.610822+00:00, the one route call edge_logs saw. MECHANISM, read from next@14.2.35's own source, not the docs: app-route/module.js handles dynamic = force-dynamic by setting only staticGenerationStore.forceDynamic, and revalidate then defaults to false; patch-fetch.js's auto-no-cache for an Authorization-header or POST fetch requires the store's revalidate === 0, so the supabase-js POST was auto cache with revalidate false, a one-year entry. WHY platform-snapshot differed: supabase-prometheus.ts:629 makes a no-store fetch BEFORE its get_supabase_cpu_max RPC at :720, which lowers the store's revalidate to 0, and every later authed fetch in that request is then auto no cache; this route's RPC was its first and only fetch. FIX: 07d193a5 — createAdminClient takes an optional { fetch } (default unchanged for all other callers), noStoreFetch forces cache no-store on the global patched fetch at call time, the route passes it and logs elapsed_ms beside at=. Deployed via main at 02:17:11 UTC 09-23. pipeline_state.cron_watchdog_vercel, first row ever: at 02:20:19.153 (read 02:20:51), 02:22:18.573 (read 02:22:58), 02:24:18.775 (read 02:25:33). Record commit fa701d86. The systemic version of this (36 GET-only admin-client routes with no no-store signal) is filed as [[FIX-1214]]. <!--id:FIX-1208-->
- 🟡 S — **receipts:daily against LOCAL overwrites the day's PROD receipt at the same nominal-day path** — receipts-daily.ts resolves its output path from the nominal day alone (receipts-daily.ts:1048-1053), so a LOCAL run and the nightly PROD run write the same docs/receipts/YYYY-MM-DD.md + .json. The target (--local / --prod / auto) selects the DATABASE and nothing else, which means an operator running data:receipts:daily locally to look at something silently overwrites the day's committed prod receipt with local-Docker numbers, and the diff looks like a legitimate update. cc-142 restored 2026-09-22 from HEAD by hand after exactly this. The nightly path (nightly.yml:564, data:receipts:daily:ci --prod) is not at risk; only the human-invoked local one is. Fix: when the resolved target is LOCAL and no --out was given, write under docs/receipts/local/ (gitignored) and say so in one line; --out still overrides; bands.json keeps loading from the canonical dir so a redirected run does not silently read no-band for every job. Cross-refs [[FIX-1176]] (the receipts writer) and [[FIX-1198]] (the sibling cwd-relative path bug in the same resolver family). <!--id:FIX-1209-->
- 🔴 M — **The FIX-1074 drain's entity_connections stale-money-edge delete has never worked — two schema errors, hidden by a test that regexes the SQL string** — fr-rewrite-drain.ts step 1 (delete stale money edges) is wrong in two independent ways and has never executed successfully. (1) e.connection_type = ANY($2::text[]) compares an ENUM column to text and raises 42883 operator does not exist: connection_type = text. (2) e.evidence_id does not exist — the column is evidence_ids, uuid[]. Error (1) aborts the parse before (2) is reached, so only the first is visible. It survived because the single test asserted on the SQL TEXT with a regex (assert.match(del, /evidence_id = d.fr_id/)) and never executed it; a PREPARE against local Docker reproduces the failure instantly and always would have. Surfaced on prod in cc-144 at 2026-09-22 09:02 UTC, where it threw AFTER the landing's DELETE of 675 financial_relationships rows had already COMMITTED, leaving 675 stale entity_connections donation edges for one official (Everton Blair, overstating by 604,079 dollars). Those edges are unreclaimable by any scheduled work: the EC donations arm is incremental on financial_relationships.updated_at and a DELETED row has no updated_at, so nothing short of a full rebuild_entity_connections() will ever remove them. This is the [[FIX-1074]] hazard realised through a code defect rather than an omission — the landing owned its derived debt, tried to pay it, and the payment instruction was invalid. evidence_ids being an array also changes the semantics: an edge may only be deleted when EVERY id it rests on was deleted (<@), since one that lost only part of its evidence would take live evidence with it; such an edge is now counted and reported instead. Fixed here with the cast, the array containment predicate, a partial-staleness warning, and a test that PREPAREs the statement against the real schema. Cross-refs [[FIX-1106]], [[FIX-544]], [[FIX-693]]. <!--id:FIX-1210-->
- 🟡 S — **One entity_connections money edge overstates by $654 — it aggregated two FR rows and only one was deleted, and no scoped rebuild exists to recompute it** — Surfaced by [[FIX-1210]]'s new partial-staleness warning on its first prod run (cc-144, 2026-09-22 10:19 UTC). entity_connections edge db99c5ef-62f0-409c-a1cd-04b3684baeb9 (donor 6d1b3de4 -> official d798e047 Britta Aguirre) carries evidence_count 2 / amount_cents 215399, aggregating her fec_bulk_pac row (65400) and her fec_bulk_ie row (149999). The FIX-1106 pac apply deleted the pac row, so the edge now overstates by 654 dollars and should read 149999 with evidence_count 1. It was deliberately NOT deleted: deleting it would drop the live IE evidence and make that money invisible, which is worse than an overstatement. It was also not hand-patched, because strength is a derived numeric(4,3) whose formula is not obvious from the call site and writing derived data one cannot derive is how a visible error becomes a subtle one. Nothing scheduled will fix it: the EC donations arm is incremental on financial_relationships.updated_at, the surviving IE row's updated_at is old, and the deleted row has none, so only a full rebuild_entity_connections() reaches it — and there is no per-entity donations rebuild (the rebuild_entity_connections_* family covers gifts/holds/investigation/lobbying/oversight/votes, not donations). The durable fix is a scoped donation-edge recompute that the drain can call for exactly this case; until then any FR deletion that hits a multi-evidence edge leaves this residue. Cross-refs [[FIX-1106]], [[FIX-1074]], [[FIX-544]]. <!--id:FIX-1211-->
